Given the increasing use of deep learning in relational settings, providing human-understandable explanations is crucial for trust, debugging, and knowledge discovery. The framework allows users to understand what the model relies upon most to make its decision and to verify it is not relying on any spurious data.
    The experimental evaluation using RelBench further strengthens the paper by demonstrating the practical utility of the proposed approach on real-world datasets and tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Conceptually Strong Framework:** The framework is well-motivated, theoretically grounded, and offers flexibility in terms of explanation granularity and trade-offs.
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  Addresses a practical problem of explaining deep learning models when deployed on relational data.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental evaluation on the RelBench collection showcases the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Heuristic Algorithms:** Provides a clever heuristic that allows the algorithm to run in a realistic timeframe compared to an exhaustive search.
    *   **Language Support:** Supports SQL which is familiar to many users of the database and offers flexibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Heuristic Nature of Implementation:** The heuristic algorithms, while necessary for efficiency, potentially limit the framework's ability to find the absolute "best" explanations according to the soft determinacy criterion. Further research could explore more principled approximations of the soft determinacy objective.
    *   **Limited SQL Fragment Support:**  The focus on projections, foreign-key joins, and selections, while a reasonable starting point, could be expanded to encompass more complex SQL constructs to express richer explanation views (e.g., aggregate queries, window functions).
    *   **Instance-Agnostic Focus:** Focusing on instance-agnostic explanations simplifies the problem, but it also limits the ability to explain individual predictions in a fine-grained way. Expanding the framework to support instance-specific views would significantly enhance its expressiveness and applicability. While instance-agnostic explanations is a limitation, it has been clearly indicated as a known limitation, and they are actively working to address it.
    *   **Dependence on Database Perturbation:** Evaluating soft determinacy requires perturbing the database, which can be computationally expensive and requires careful consideration of the perturbation distribution. While the authors explore different perturbation strategies, a more rigorous analysis of their impact on explanation quality would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in explainable machine learning, particularly in relational settings.  It provides a strong foundation for developing more sophisticated explanation techniques that leverage database concepts and techniques. Also, it can influence the use of RelBench as the standard for deep learning on relational databases. The general framework might also inspire new ways to extract the most important data from complex relations.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper is a significant contribution to the field of explainable AI and relational deep learning. The core idea of using database views as explanations is highly innovative and has strong practical relevance. The empirical evaluation is thorough and demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach. However, the heuristic nature of the implementation, the limited support for SQL fragments, and the focus on instance-agnostic explanations prevent it from reaching a higher score. Future work that addresses these limitations would significantly enhance the paper's impact.

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 8/10

### Prompt Pirates Need a Map: Stealing Seeds helps Stealing Prompts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09488v1)
- **Authors**: Felix Mächtle, Ashwath Shetty, Jonas Sander, Nils Loose, Sören Pirk, Thomas Eisenbarth
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have significantly advanced text-to-image generation, enabling the creation of highly realistic images conditioned on textual prompts and seeds. Given the considerable intellectual and economic value embedded in such prompts, prompt theft poses a critical security and privacy concern. In this paper, we investigate prompt-stealing attacks targeting diffusion models. We reveal that numerical optimization-based prompt recovery methods are fundamentally limited as they do not account for the initial random noise used during image generation. We identify and exploit a noise-generation vulnerability (CWE-339), prevalent in major image-generation frameworks, originating from PyTorch's restriction of seed values to a range of $2^{32}$ when generating the initial random noise on CPUs. Through a large-scale empirical analysis conducted on images shared via the popular platform CivitAI, we demonstrate that approximately 95% of these images' seed values can be effectively brute-forced in 140 minutes per seed using our seed-recovery tool, SeedSnitch. Leveraging the recovered seed, we propose PromptPirate, a genetic algorithm-based optimization method explicitly designed for prompt stealing. PromptPirate surpasses state-of-the-art methods, i.e., PromptStealer, P2HP, and CLIP-Interrogator, achieving an 8-11% improvement in LPIPS similarity. Furthermore, we introduce straightforward and effective countermeasures that render seed stealing, and thus optimization-based prompt stealing, ineffective. We have disclosed our findings responsibly and initiated coordinated mitigation efforts with the developers to address this critical vulnerability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the security vulnerability of prompt stealing in diffusion models. The core problem is that valuable prompts, containing intellectual property, can be reverse-engineered from generated images. The authors identify and exploit a critical vulnerability (CWE-339) in how diffusion models generate initial noise: the use of a limited (32-bit) seed space in PyTorch's CPU-based random number generator (PRNG). This limitation allows attackers to brute-force the seed, recover the initial noise state, and significantly improve the accuracy of prompt-stealing attacks. They introduce SeedSnitch, a seed-recovery tool, and PromptPirate, a genetic algorithm-based prompt-stealing method that leverages the recovered seed. Experimental results show that SeedSnitch can effectively recover seeds from images shared on platforms like CivitAI, and PromptPirate outperforms existing prompt-stealing techniques significantly. Finally, the paper proposes countermeasures to mitigate the vulnerability by suggesting the adoption of cryptographically secure PRNGs with larger seed spaces.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several novel aspects:
    *   It identifies and exploits a practical and widespread security vulnerability (CWE-339) in diffusion models related to limited seed space in PRNGs.
    *   It demonstrates empirically the crucial role of the initial noise seed in prompt stealing, which had been largely overlooked by previous research.
    *   It proposes SeedSnitch, a seed-recovery tool, and PromptPirate, a prompt-stealing method that leverages seed knowledge, showing a significant improvement over existing techniques.
    *   It provides a detailed analysis of the seed distribution in real-world images shared on platforms like CivitAI, revealing concerning patterns of seed usage.
    *   It clearly states and responsibly discloses a pervasive CWE-339 vulnerability in a wide range of image diffusion model implementations.

*   **Significance:** The research is significant for several reasons:
    *   It raises awareness about a critical security and privacy concern in the context of generative AI. Prompt stealing can undermine the intellectual property and economic value associated with carefully crafted prompts.
    *   It provides practical methods for both exploiting and mitigating the identified vulnerability, offering valuable insights for developers and users of diffusion models.
    *   The findings emphasize the importance of using secure PRNGs with sufficiently large seed spaces in generative AI applications, highlighting a broader security concern beyond just diffusion models.
    *   Their analysis of real-world seed distribution provides key input and valuable insight into practical security of real-world implementations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and easy to follow.
    *   The methodology is sound and the experiments are well-designed.
    *   The empirical results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive analysis of the problem, including both exploitation and mitigation strategies.
    *   The responsible disclosure of the vulnerability to the affected developers is commendable.
    *   The open sourcing of SeedSnitch and PromptPirate promotes transparency and facilitates further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the proposed PromptPirate outperforms existing methods, the reported LPIPS score of 0.52, with a known subject, still indicates that there is room for improvement in recapturing the full quality of the prompts.
    *   While identifying the broader CWE-339 vulnerability is appreciated, future work could include actively testing different random-number-generating techniques to suggest potential countermeasures to the identified vulnerability.
    *   The mitigation strategies could be discussed further to understand the practicality of integration in different frameworks.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of generative AI security.
    *   It will likely prompt developers to address the identified vulnerability in their diffusion model implementations.
    *   It will raise awareness among users about the potential risks associated with sharing images generated by diffusion models.
    *   It will encourage further research into the security and privacy of generative AI models.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper provides a significant contribution by identifying, exploiting, and proposing mitigation strategies for a practically relevant vulnerability in diffusion models. The methodology is sound, results are compelling, and responsible disclosure is commendable. This paper has immediate practical application. While there's always room for refining the proposed techniques, the paper presents a clear advance.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Mixture of Semantics Transmission for Generative AI-Enabled Semantic Communication Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09499v1)
- **Authors**: Junjie Ni, Tong Wu, Zhiyong Chen, Yin Xu, Meixia Tao, Wenjun Zhang
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a mixture of semantics (MoS) transmission strategy for wireless semantic communication systems based on generative artificial intelligence (AI). At the transmitter, we divide an image into regions of interest (ROI) and reigons of non-interest (RONI) to extract their semantic information respectively. Semantic information of ROI can be allocated more bandwidth, while RONI can be represented in a compact form for transmission. At the receiver, a diffusion model reconstructs the full image using the received semantic information of ROI and RONI. Compared to existing generative AI-based methods, MoS enables more efficient use of channel resources by balancing visual fidelity and semantic relevance. Experimental results demonstrate that appropriate ROI-RONI allocation is critical. The MoS achieves notable performance gains in peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) of ROI and CLIP score of RONI.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a "Mixture of Semantics" (MoS) transmission strategy for wireless semantic communication systems using generative AI. The key idea is to divide an image into Regions of Interest (ROI) and Regions of Non-Interest (RONI), applying different encoding strategies to each. The ROI is encoded with a vision-based JSCC (Joint Source-Channel Coding) to preserve fidelity, while the RONI is converted to text using a vision-language model (BLIP) and then encoded with a text-based JSCC for more efficient transmission. At the receiver, a diffusion model reconstructs the full image using the received ROI data and the semantic information extracted from the RONI text. The paper emphasizes balancing the transmission ratio between ROI and RONI to optimize performance, showing improvements in PSNR (Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio) for the ROI and CLIP score for the RONI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of selectively applying different encoding strategies (vision-based vs. text-based) based on the importance of different image regions (ROI vs. RONI) is a reasonable and potentially effective approach. Leveraging a vision-language model to compress the RONI into text before transmission is also innovative within the context of semantic communications. Using SAM for image segmentation and diffusion models for reconstruction are not entirely novel on their own, but their integration within the proposed framework represents a new approach. The concept of mixture semantics has not been rigorously explored within the field, and this work could potentially be one of the first efforts in this direction.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in semantic communication: efficient resource allocation. By prioritizing the ROI and using a more compact representation for the RONI, it aims to improve overall image quality under bandwidth constraints. The experimental results support this claim, showing improvements in PSNR and CLIP score. If the performance gains are substantial in realistic scenarios, the MoS strategy could significantly impact the design of future wireless semantic communication systems. The idea could potentially inspire further research into more sophisticated semantic decomposition and encoding techniques, tailoring resource allocation to the specific needs of different parts of an image or other data types.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured paper.
    *   The proposed MoS strategy is well-motivated and explained.
    *   The use of SAM and diffusion models is appropriate for the task.
    *   The experimental results are promising, showing improvements over baselines.
    *   Addresses the challenging problem of balancing fidelity and efficiency in semantic communication.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experimental setup is adequate, the results might benefit from more extensive evaluations using different datasets.
    *   The paper may lack a thorough comparison of the computational overhead and latency of the proposed approach compared to alternative strategies. The paper mentions this in the results, but a more extensive analysis is needed.
    *   The paper could benefit from further discussion on how to automatically and adaptively determine the optimal ROI/RONI segmentation based on user needs and channel conditions. Manually determining this allocation might limit its practical applicability.
    *   The potential robustness to channel impairments (e.g., noise, fading) for the *text* part of the transmission path needs more discussion.

*   **Score Justification:**

The paper has merit, is well written, and has some nice insights. The potential improvements in PSNR and CLIP Score are encouraging. However, the method has a few limitations that lower its score. The method requires the image segmentation to be done at the transmitter which increases the computational overhead and therefore requires more power.
The channel robustness for the RONI text transfer needs more improvement and the ROI selection is still being selected manually. Therefore, I am giving it a moderate score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.IT
- **Score**: 6/10

### DeMeVa at LeWiDi-2025: Modeling Perspectives with In-Context Learning and Label Distribution Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09524v1)
- **Authors**: Daniil Ignatev, Nan Li, Hugh Mee Wong, Anh Dang, Shane Kaszefski Yaschuk
- **Abstract**: This system paper presents the DeMeVa team's approaches to the third edition of the Learning with Disagreements shared task (LeWiDi 2025; Leonardelli et al., 2025). We explore two directions: in-context learning (ICL) with large language models, where we compare example sampling strategies; and label distribution learning (LDL) methods with RoBERTa (Liu et al., 2019b), where we evaluate several fine-tuning methods. Our contributions are twofold: (1) we show that ICL can effectively predict annotator-specific annotations (perspectivist annotations), and that aggregating these predictions into soft labels yields competitive performance; and (2) we argue that LDL methods are promising for soft label predictions and merit further exploration by the perspectivist community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents the DeMeVa team's approach to the LeWiDi 2025 shared task, focusing on learning from disagreements in NLP. The team explored two primary directions: in-context learning (ICL) with large language models (LLMs) and label distribution learning (LDL) with RoBERTa. For ICL, they experimented with different example sampling strategies (similarity-based and stratified label-based).  For LDL, they fine-tuned RoBERTa using ordinal label distribution learning and a method for predicting population-level label distributions via clustering. The paper demonstrates that ICL can effectively predict annotator-specific annotations and that LDL methods show promise for soft label prediction. The best results were achieved using ICL, where models calibrated by label-based sampling can accurately mimic annotators' specific behavior and also provides interesting observations for the community.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a reasonable exploration of existing techniques (ICL and LDL) applied to a novel task (LeWiDi 2025, focusing on learning from disagreements).  While the individual components (ICL, RoBERTa fine-tuning, specific LDL methods) are not groundbreaking on their own, their application in this specific context, aiming to model annotator perspectives, adds a layer of novelty. The comparison of different sampling strategies in ICL is also a valuable contribution. However, the ICL part is very close to baselines explored in prior work regarding model and sampling strategy.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its address of the crucial problem of handling disagreements in NLP annotation. By exploring perspectivist modeling, the work acknowledges the inherent subjectivity in many NLP tasks and tries to learn the annotations as a distribution rather than aiming at a single "gold" annotation. This direction is becoming increasingly important as NLP tackles more complex and nuanced tasks. The comparative analysis of ICL and LDL provides practical insights for researchers working in this area. The paper also highlights the potential of LDL approaches, which have not been widely adopted in NLP but offer promising avenues for modeling label distributions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic evaluation of different ICL sampling strategies for perspectivist modeling.
    *   Exploration of LDL methods in the context of NLP disagreement learning.
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation of the experiments and results.
    *   The paper brings a valuable perspective about how common approaches used in other fields (e.g. probabilistic and distributional techniques in computer vision) could impact the NLP field.
    *   The authors provide concrete and interesting observations regarding the behavior of LLMs when dealing with disagreements, showing that LLMs often tend to predict agreement even in cases where annotators do not agree.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvements achieved with ICL are incremental and don't demonstrate a significant breakthrough.
    *   The LDL approach results are not competitive with the ICL approach, suggesting that more exploration is needed in this direction.
    *   The datasets have different properties (size, number of annotators), which limits the generalization of the findings.
    *   Lack of a deeper analysis of why specific LDL methods work or don't work for this task. The discussion is more descriptive than analytical.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by:
    *   Encouraging researchers to explore LDL methods for disagreement learning.
    *   Motivating further research on ICL sampling strategies for perspectivist modeling.
    *   Raising awareness about the importance of handling annotation disagreements in NLP.

**Overall:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to the field by exploring and comparing different approaches to learning from disagreements in NLP. While the improvements are not transformative, the systematic evaluation and practical insights provided make it a valuable addition to the literature.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Prompting the Market? A Large-Scale Meta-Analysis of GenAI in Finance NLP (2022-2025)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09544v1)
- **Authors**: Paolo Pedinotti, Peter Baumann, Nathan Jessurun, Leslie Barrett, Enrico Santus
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have rapidly reshaped financial NLP, enabling new tasks and driving a proliferation of datasets and diversification of data sources. Yet, this transformation has outpaced traditional surveys. In this paper, we present MetaGraph, a generalizable methodology for extracting knowledge graphs from scientific literature and analyzing them to obtain a structured, queryable view of research trends. We define an ontology for financial NLP research and apply an LLM-based extraction pipeline to 681 papers (2022-2025), enabling large-scale, data-driven analysis. MetaGraph reveals three key phases: early LLM adoption and task/dataset innovation; critical reflection on LLM limitations; and growing integration of peripheral techniques into modular systems. This structured view offers both practitioners and researchers a clear understanding of how financial NLP has evolved - highlighting emerging trends, shifting priorities, and methodological shifts-while also demonstrating a reusable approach for mapping scientific progress in other domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "MetaGraph," a methodology that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically construct Knowledge Graphs (KGs) from scientific literature, specifically within the financial NLP domain.  The goal is to provide a structured, queryable view of research trends in this rapidly evolving field. They define an ontology for financial NLP research and apply an LLM-based extraction pipeline to 681 papers published between 2022 and 2025. The MetaGraph reveals three key phases: early LLM adoption/task innovation, critical reflection on LLM limitations, and the integration of peripheral techniques. The paper contributes a generalizable pipeline for extracting insights from scientific literature, maps field evolution, and releases the resulting knowledge graph.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its methodology for systematically analyzing a large corpus of research papers *using* LLMs. While LLMs are increasingly used in financial NLP, employing them to *analyze* the field itself provides a fresh perspective.  The approach of creating a queryable knowledge graph from scientific publications to understand trends is innovative.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it offers a data-driven, quantitative analysis of the impact of GenAI (specifically LLMs) on the financial NLP landscape. Traditional surveys often lag behind rapidly changing fields like this. MetaGraph allows for identification of emerging trends, shifting priorities, and methodological shifts, which is valuable for both researchers and practitioners. The release of the MetaGraph as an open resource further enhances its significance. The identification of key phases (early adoption, reflection on limitations, and system integration) provides a valuable framework for understanding the evolution of the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Methodology:** The MetaGraph methodology is well-defined and appears to be generalizable to other domains beyond financial NLP.
    *   **Data-Driven Analysis:** The quantitative nature of the analysis provides concrete evidence for trends.
    *   **Timeliness:** The analysis covers a recent and crucial period (2022-2025) in the evolution of NLP.
    *   **Open Resource:**  The release of the knowledge graph ensures reproducibility and allows for further exploration by other researchers.
    *   **Addresses a Gap:** Effectively bridges the gap between traditional narrative surveys and the rapid change in generative AI research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependency:**  The reliance on LLMs introduces potential biases and inaccuracies in the extraction process. While human-in-the-loop validation is mentioned, the impact of LLM limitations on the extracted graph could be more explicitly addressed. The quality of the LLM used for extraction directly influences the MetaGraph's accuracy.
    *   **Ontology Dependence:**  The manually defined ontology shapes the analysis, and may unintentionally introduce bias in the analysis of the literature (as stated in the paper). The authors mention limitations around taxonomy and extraction bias, but how this skews the insights presented would be a valuable addition.
    *   **Limited Scope of Insights:** While the paper highlights key trends, a deeper dive into the *reasons* behind these trends or the *impact* of specific changes on real-world financial applications could enhance the significance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The methodology and the resulting MetaGraph have the potential to influence future research directions in financial NLP and provide a template for analyzing the evolution of other AI-driven fields. It can also guide resource allocation and strategic decision-making for organizations involved in financial NLP research and development.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper offers a valuable and novel contribution to the field by providing a systematic, data-driven approach to understanding the impact of GenAI on financial NLP. The methodology is well-defined and generalizable. The limitations stemming from relying on LLMs and a manually-defined ontology are acknowledged. However, a more detailed analysis of the potential impact of these limitations would enhance the paper. The actionable insight gleaned by the authors would also be a boon for researchers and industry practitioners looking for a high-level view of the field's research efforts.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Video Diffusion Transformer Training by Multi-Feature Fusion and Alignment from Self-Supervised Vision Encoders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09547v1)
- **Authors**: Dohun Lee, Hyeonho Jeong, Jiwook Kim, Duygu Ceylan, Jong Chul Ye
- **Abstract**: Video diffusion models have advanced rapidly in the recent years as a result of series of architectural innovations (e.g., diffusion transformers) and use of novel training objectives (e.g., flow matching). In contrast, less attention has been paid to improving the feature representation power of such models. In this work, we show that training video diffusion models can benefit from aligning the intermediate features of the video generator with feature representations of pre-trained vision encoders. We propose a new metric and conduct an in-depth analysis of various vision encoders to evaluate their discriminability and temporal consistency, thereby assessing their suitability for video feature alignment. Based on the analysis, we present Align4Gen which provides a novel multi-feature fusion and alignment method integrated into video diffusion model training. We evaluate Align4Gen both for unconditional and class-conditional video generation tasks and show that it results in improved video generation as quantified by various metrics. Full video results are available on our project page: https://align4gen.github.io/align4gen/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Align4Gen, a novel framework that improves video diffusion transformer training by aligning intermediate features of the video generator with feature representations from pre-trained vision encoders. It proposes a new metric, the Intra-Inter Consistency Ratio (IICR), to evaluate the discriminability and temporal consistency of vision encoders and uses this analysis to select DINOv2 and SAM2.1 Hiera as guides. Align4Gen incorporates a multi-feature fusion and alignment strategy into video diffusion training, and experimental results on unconditional and class-conditional video generation tasks demonstrate improved video quality.  The key idea is that pre-trained vision models can provide valuable guidance for training video diffusion models, leading to more discriminative features and higher quality generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in the specific combination of ideas: (1) using pre-trained vision encoders (specifically DINOv2 and SAM2.1 Hiera) to guide video diffusion models, (2) introducing the IICR metric for evaluating the suitability of vision encoders for this task, and (3) implementing a multi-feature fusion and alignment strategy.  While the general idea of using pre-trained models as guidance isn't entirely new (REPA did it for image diffusion), the application to *video*, the *IICR metric*, and the *multi-feature fusion* aspect provide sufficient novelty. The IICR metric, in particular, appears to be a thoughtful contribution to the field.  The experimental design appears comprehensive with thorough ablations.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance stems from the possibility of making video diffusion models more data-efficient and improving their output quality. Aligning with powerful, pre-trained vision encoders *could* lead to models that require less training data and generate more realistic and consistent videos. The reported results support this claim, showing improved FVD, FID, and IS scores, as well as faster convergence.  The faster convergence claim is particularly significant. The use of multi-feature fusion is a significant and non-trivial extension of the REPA approach.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Motivation and Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the problem it addresses and motivates the proposed solution well.
    *   **Well-Defined Metric (IICR):** The IICR metric is a significant contribution, providing a principled way to select and evaluate pre-trained vision encoders.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents thorough experimental results with ablations, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed method and justifying design choices.
    *   **Faster Convergence:** The experiments showing that the approach exhibits faster convergence of video generation tasks is of high significance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Specific Encoders:**  While DINOv2 and SAM2.1 Hiera are strong encoders, the paper's reliance on these specific models *could* limit its generalizability.  Further exploration with other vision encoders would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Text-to-Video Challenge:**  The limitation experienced extending the approach to text-to-video generation shows that more study is required.
    *   **Limited Novelty of Alignment Strategy:** The alignment loss function (cosine distance) and the general approach of aligning features aren't fundamentally novel in themselves.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in video generation, particularly in exploring the use of pre-trained vision encoders for guiding diffusion models. The IICR metric could become a valuable tool for researchers in this area. The idea of using multi-feature fusion is significant, and the approach exhibits fast convergence.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to improving video diffusion transformer training.  The IICR metric and the multi-feature fusion strategy are significant contributions. The comprehensive experiments and clear writing support the claims. The limitation observed with extending the approach to text-to-video tasks does, however, slightly reduce the overall significance.

Score: 7.5
The score reflects the paper's significant contributions to the video generation field, specifically the IICR metric and the successful application of multi-feature fusion with pre-trained vision encoders. The approach also exhibits fast convergence for training video generation tasks. While there are weaknesses, these do not significantly detract from the core contributions. The challenges faced during the application to text-to-video tasks reveals the need for further research, but do not greatly reduce the paper's overall potential impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Finite Scalar Quantization Enables Redundant and Transmission-Robust Neural Audio Compression at Low Bit-rates
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09550v2)
- **Authors**: Harry Julian, Rachel Beeson, Lohith Konathala, Johanna Ulin, Jiameng Gao
- **Abstract**: Neural Audio Codecs (NACs) have become increasingly adopted in speech processing tasks due to their excellent rate-distortion performance and compatibility with Large Language Models (LLMs) as discrete feature representations for audio generation. While most existing codecs rely on Residual Vector Quantization (RVQ), Finite Scalar Quantization (FSQ) has recently emerged as a compelling alternative that simplifies training and natively supports single codebooks. We introduce NeuCodec, an FSQ-based NAC, and show that FSQ encodes baked-in redundancy which produces an encoding which is robust when transmitted through noisy channels. First, through an encoder distillation experiment, we show that two different encoders can learn to encode identical audio into vastly different code sequences whilst maintaining comparable reconstruction quality with the same quantizer and decoder. Second, we demonstrate that FSQ has vastly superior bit-level perturbation robustness by comparing the performance of RVQ and FSQ codecs when simulating the transmission of code sequences through a noisy channel.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Finite Scalar Quantization (FSQ) in Neural Audio Codecs (NACs) for low bit-rate audio compression.  The authors introduce NeuCodec, an FSQ-based NAC, and demonstrate its inherent redundancy, making it robust to bit-level perturbations during transmission. They show through encoder distillation that different encoders can produce dissimilar code sequences for the same audio while maintaining reconstruction quality. They also compare FSQ and Residual Vector Quantization (RVQ) codecs in a noisy channel simulation, finding FSQ codecs perform better under perturbation. They posit that FSQ's inherent properties, by distributing information across codewords, contribute to this robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of using FSQ in NACs isn't entirely novel, but the specific investigation into its perturbation robustness and comparison with RVQ in this context offers a valuable contribution. The distillation experiment is a useful way to highlight the flexibility afforded by the FSQ quantization space and the built-in redundancy. The simulation of noisy channels to assess codec performance is a common technique, but the specific application to compare FSQ and RVQ in NACs is unique.

**Significance:**  The paper's findings have practical implications for low bit-rate audio compression in scenarios where transmission errors are likely.  FSQ's robustness could be advantageous in applications such as voice over IP (VoIP), mobile communication, and audio streaming over unreliable networks. Demonstrating robustness allows to further develop FSQ-based approaches in Neural Audio Codecs field, encouraging its implementation and further research on the subject.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is logically organized and clearly written. The experiments are well-defined, and the results are presented understandably.
*   **Strong Experimental Validation:** The encoder distillation and perturbation experiments provide compelling evidence for FSQ's robustness.
*   **Relevant Comparisons:** The comparison of FSQ with the widely used RVQ codecs is crucial for establishing the practical value of the proposed approach.
*   **Reproducibility-Driven:** Detailed information about the datasets, training parameters, and evaluation metrics used throughout the experiments.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Analysis of the Distillation Experiment:** Whilst the paper clearly demonstrates the reconstruction quality and performance similarity between the different encoders, it would benefit from a more in-depth analysis of exactly *how* the output projections are re-aligned in the implicit codebook to obtain reconstruction quality.
*   **Limited Scope of Noisy Channel Simulation:**  While the binary symmetric channel model is a good starting point, it's a simplified representation of real-world communication channels.  Further investigation into more complex channel models (e.g., fading channels) would strengthen the claims.
*   **Potential Limitations with Codebook Size:** The robustness may be tied to the codebook size used for FSQ. A smaller codebook might not exhibit the same degree of redundancy. Exploration of how performance scales with the codebook size may be relevant to strengthen the claims of the experiment.

**Justification for Score:**

This is a solid paper with a well-defined research question, appropriate methodology, and results that suggest that FSQ offers advantages for audio compression in noisy environments. The distilled encoder experiment and robust data simulation reinforce its contribution.  However, the lack of extensive analysis regarding the alignment of the projections of the model after the encoding step, the simple model in simulating noisy data and the dependency of robustness with codebook size affect the validity of the claims being made. Despite all this, the paper gives strong indications to further the state-of-the-art of research regarding robustness of NACs and thus, warrants a reasonably good score.

### Fluent but Unfeeling: The Emotional Blind Spots of Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09593v1)
- **Authors**: Bangzhao Shu, Isha Joshi, Melissa Karnaze, Anh C. Pham, Ishita Kakkar, Sindhu Kothe, Arpine Hovasapian, Mai ElSherief
- **Abstract**: The versatility of Large Language Models (LLMs) in natural language understanding has made them increasingly popular in mental health research. While many studies explore LLMs' capabilities in emotion recognition, a critical gap remains in evaluating whether LLMs align with human emotions at a fine-grained level. Existing research typically focuses on classifying emotions into predefined, limited categories, overlooking more nuanced expressions. To address this gap, we introduce EXPRESS, a benchmark dataset curated from Reddit communities featuring 251 fine-grained, self-disclosed emotion labels. Our comprehensive evaluation framework examines predicted emotion terms and decomposes them into eight basic emotions using established emotion theories, enabling a fine-grained comparison. Systematic testing of prevalent LLMs under various prompt settings reveals that accurately predicting emotions that align with human self-disclosed emotions remains challenging. Qualitative analysis further shows that while certain LLMs generate emotion terms consistent with established emotion theories and definitions, they sometimes fail to capture contextual cues as effectively as human self-disclosures. These findings highlight the limitations of LLMs in fine-grained emotion alignment and offer insights for future research aimed at enhancing their contextual understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EXPRESS, a new benchmark dataset and evaluation framework for assessing the fine-grained emotional understanding capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The dataset comprises Reddit posts with self-disclosed emotions, which are masked and then predicted by LLMs. The framework goes beyond simple emotion classification by decomposing emotions into basic dimensions based on theories like Plutchik's wheel and evaluating predictions at both lexical and dimensional levels. The authors tested various LLMs under different prompt settings (zero-shot, few-shot, Chain-of-Thought) and found that accurately predicting human self-disclosed emotions remains a significant challenge. They also conducted a qualitative analysis showing that while LLMs can generate theoretically consistent emotion terms, they sometimes miss contextual cues compared to human self-disclosures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Novelty:** The EXPRESS dataset is a significant contribution. Existing benchmarks often rely on crowdsourced or expert-labeled data, which can be less ecologically valid than self-disclosed emotions. The scale and fine-grained nature of the emotion labels (251) are also novel.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The evaluation framework is well-designed, including lexical accuracy, decomposed emotion vector accuracy, and a human evaluation component. The dimensional approach is a valuable way to assess alignment even when the exact emotion words don't match.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Exploration:** The paper systematically investigates the impact of different prompting strategies (few-shot learning, CoT) on emotion recognition. The finding that CoT degrades performance in this context is interesting and warrants further investigation.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The human evaluation adds depth to the quantitative results, providing insights into the types of errors LLMs make and the contextual understanding they lack. The finding that experts *slightly* preferred LLM-generated emotion terms was unexpected.
    *   **Open Resource:** Releasing the dataset and code is a commendable practice that will facilitate future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reddit Data Bias:** The dataset is based on Reddit data, which has known demographic biases (young, male-dominated, North American/European). This limits the generalizability of the findings to other populations and contexts.
    *   **Limited Human Evaluation Scope:** While valuable, the human evaluation is based on a relatively small sample of 213 examples. More extensive human evaluation would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Lack of Task-Specific Fine-Tuning:** The paper focuses on evaluating pre-trained LLMs without task-specific fine-tuning. It is unclear if fine-tuning on EXPRESS could significantly improve performance and change the observed trends.
    *   **Definition of Performance** The dataset only utilizes exact matches to the emotion lexicon, ignoring potential overlap.
    *   **Low Interrater Agreement** The limited agreement of the judges brings into question the efficacy of the comparison, and potentially demonstrates the nuance that exists in emotion analysis.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The contribution is significant due to the inherent need to analyze and synthesize affect in LLMs given their deployment in sensitive contexts. EXPRESS offers a unique resource for research on fine-grained emotion alignment.
    *   The findings challenge the common assumption that simply increasing model size leads to better emotional understanding. The observed sensitivity to model architecture and family is important.
    *   The paper offers valuable insights for developing emotion-aware AI systems, particularly in mental health applications, as well as for designing training strategies for future LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   EXPRESS could become a widely used benchmark for emotion recognition research.
    *   The findings regarding CoT prompting could influence the design of prompts for other socially situated tasks.
    *   The paper's focus on fine-grained emotion alignment could shift the field away from coarse emotion classification toward more nuanced approaches.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a novel and valuable contribution with the EXPRESS dataset and comprehensive evaluation framework. While the data source (Reddit) introduces some limitations, the scale and richness of the dataset, combined with the insights from the quantitative and qualitative analyses, represent a solid advance. The finding regarding the efficacy of Chain-of-Thought prompting demonstrates that the methodology is well defined, and that CoT does not apply in this situation.

### How much are LLMs changing the language of academic papers after ChatGPT? A multi-database and full text analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09596v1)
- **Authors**: Kayvan Kousha, Mike Thelwall
- **Abstract**: This study investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) are influencing the language of academic papers by tracking 12 LLM-associated terms across six major scholarly databases (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, PubMed Central (PMC), Dimensions, and OpenAlex) from 2015 to 2024. Using over 2.4 million PMC open-access publications (2021-July 2025), we also analysed full texts to assess changes in the frequency and co-occurrence of these terms before and after ChatGPT's initial public release. Across databases, delve (+1,500%), underscore (+1,000%), and intricate (+700%) had the largest increases between 2022 and 2024. Growth in LLM-term usage was much higher in STEM fields than in social sciences and arts and humanities. In PMC full texts, the proportion of papers using underscore six or more times increased by over 10,000% from 2022 to 2025, followed by intricate (+5,400%) and meticulous (+2,800%). Nearly half of all 2024 PMC papers using any LLM term also included underscore, compared with only 3%-14% of papers before ChatGPT in 2022. Papers using one LLM term are now much more likely to include other terms. For example, in 2024, underscore strongly correlated with pivotal (0.449) and delve (0.311), compared with very weak associations in 2022 (0.032 and 0.018, respectively). These findings provide the first large-scale evidence based on full-text publications and multiple databases that some LLM-related terms are now being used much more frequently and together. The rapid uptake of LLMs to support scholarly publishing is a welcome development reducing the language barrier to academic publishing for non-English speakers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT are influencing the language used in academic papers. The authors track the prevalence of 12 LLM-associated terms across six major scholarly databases (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, PubMed Central, Dimensions, and OpenAlex) from 2015 to 2024.  They also analyze the full text of over 2.4 million PMC open-access publications to assess changes in term frequency and co-occurrence before and after ChatGPT's release.  The study finds a substantial increase in the use of specific terms (e.g., "delve," "underscore," "intricate") post-ChatGPT, with variations across disciplines (STEM vs. social sciences/humanities).  They also observe an increased co-occurrence of these LLM-associated terms within papers, suggesting a shift in academic writing style. Finally, they assess if LLM associated terms are appearing in greater proportions in retracted papers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its **large-scale full-text analysis** across multiple databases over a relatively long period (2015-2024). While prior work has touched on the topic of LLM influence on academic writing, most studies have been smaller in scope, focusing on specific fields or abstracts. This work's approach to tracking the co-occurrence and repeated usage of selected terms is unique and offers valuable insight. The comparison to the increasing proportion of LLM terms in retracted works is somewhat novel, but likely requires more robust analysis.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant for several reasons:

    *   **Empirical evidence of LLM influence:**  The study provides strong empirical evidence that LLMs are, in fact, changing the language of academic writing, going beyond anecdotal observations.
    *   **Disciplinary variations:** The identified differences in term usage across STEM and social sciences/humanities raise important questions about how LLMs are being adopted and adapted in different research contexts.
    *   **Implications for academic publishing:** The results have implications for academic publishing, potentially leading to changes in editorial standards and the development of tools to detect or address LLM-influenced writing.
    *   **Raises questions about scholarly integrity:** The investigation into terms in retracted papers highlights concerns about the potential for misuse or over-reliance on LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large dataset and robust methodology:** The use of multiple databases and a large corpus of full-text articles lends credibility to the findings. The methods are well-defined and replicable.
    *   **Clear research questions:** The research questions are clearly articulated and addressed systematically.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper goes beyond simple frequency counts to examine co-occurrence patterns and correlations between terms.
    *   **Consideration of limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their study, such as the limited set of LLM-associated terms and the focus on English-language publications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Causality is not definitively established:** While the study shows correlations, it doesn't conclusively prove that LLMs are *causing* the observed changes. Other factors, such as evolving academic trends, could be contributing.
    *   **Term selection is subjective:** The selection of LLM-associated terms, although justified, involves some subjectivity. The rationale could be strengthened through further validation (e.g., expert consultation).
    *   **Potential for bias:** The focus on open-access PMC data could introduce bias, as it may not fully represent the publication landscape in other closed-access journals.
    *   **Lack of deeper semantic analysis:** The study focuses on term frequency and co-occurrence but doesn't delve into the *meaning* or *semantic function* of these terms within the articles.

*   **Potential influence:** The paper has the potential to stimulate further research on the impact of LLMs on academic writing and publishing. It could also inform the development of tools and guidelines for responsible LLM use in scholarly communication.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper's methodology is strong and its scope is extensive, the primary findings are primarily *observational*. It presents clear empirical trends but does not fully establish causal relationships. It has a large-scale observation of the academic writing, and highlights the trends with the changes occurring after LLM usage. However, future work will likely build on the trends to highlight and develop strategies and tools to help overcome such usages. Overall, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, but the limited causality hinders a higher assessment.

### LAVA: Language Model Assisted Verbal Autopsy for Cause-of-Death Determination
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09602v1)
- **Authors**: Yiqun T. Chen, Tyler H. McCormick, Li Liu, Abhirup Datta
- **Abstract**: Verbal autopsy (VA) is a critical tool for estimating causes of death in resource-limited settings where medical certification is unavailable. This study presents LA-VA, a proof-of-concept pipeline that combines Large Language Models (LLMs) with traditional algorithmic approaches and embedding-based classification for improved cause-of-death prediction. Using the Population Health Metrics Research Consortium (PHMRC) dataset across three age categories (Adult: 7,580; Child: 1,960; Neonate: 2,438), we evaluate multiple approaches: GPT-5 predictions, LCVA baseline, text embeddings, and meta-learner ensembles. Our results demonstrate that GPT-5 achieves the highest individual performance with average test site accuracies of 48.6% (Adult), 50.5% (Child), and 53.5% (Neonate), outperforming traditional statistical machine learning baselines by 5-10%. Our findings suggest that simple off-the-shelf LLM-assisted approaches could substantially improve verbal autopsy accuracy, with important implications for global health surveillance in low-resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LA-VA, a pipeline that combines large language models (LLMs) with traditional verbal autopsy analysis methods to improve cause-of-death (COD) prediction in resource-limited settings. The pipeline integrates GPT-5 predictions, a latent class verbal autopsy (LCVA) baseline, text embeddings, and meta-learner ensembles. Using the PHMRC dataset across adult, child, and neonate age categories, the study demonstrates that GPT-5 achieves the highest individual performance, outperforming traditional statistical machine learning baselines. The results suggest that LLM-assisted approaches can substantially improve verbal autopsy accuracy, with implications for global health surveillance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of a state-of-the-art LLM (GPT-5) to the problem of verbal autopsy, integrating it with established statistical methods. While LLMs have been applied to healthcare-related tasks, their use in COD determination from verbal autopsy data, particularly with a focus on low-resource settings, is a relatively new area. The pipeline integrating several components such as LLM, embedding and statistical model is another novelty.

*   **Significance:** Verbal autopsy is crucial in low-resource settings where medical certification of death is unavailable. Improvements in COD prediction accuracy directly translate to better public health surveillance, resource allocation, and targeted interventions. The potential to leverage LLMs for this purpose, without requiring extensive retraining or site-specific data, offers a practical advantage over traditional methods. The use of population level CSMF as a metric is also a good indication that the results are useful.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive Evaluation: The paper evaluates several approaches (LLM, LCVA, embeddings, ensembles) and uses leave-one-site-out cross-validation to assess geographic generalizability.
    *   Real-world Dataset: The use of the PHMRC dataset, a gold-standard in verbal autopsy research, provides a strong basis for evaluating performance.
    *   Focus on Low-Resource Settings: The explicit consideration of domain shift and data scarcity, which are common challenges in low-resource settings, increases the practical relevance of the findings.
    *   Careful LLM prompt design and evaluation: The paper presents careful design of the prompts and calibration methods.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on a closed-source LLM: The study relies on GPT-5, a closed-source LLM. This limits the reproducibility and accessibility of the research for other researchers, especially in settings where GPT-5 may not be available or affordable.
    *   Historical Data: The use of PHMRC data (collected 2007-2010) introduces potential biases due to changes in disease prevalence, healthcare practices, and data collection methods. Although a standardized dataset like PHMRC's is ideal to compare methods, its temporal detachment from the "real world" can be problematic.
    *   Computational/Privacy Concerns: The practical deployment of LLMs may be restricted by computational costs and privacy concerns, particularly in resource-limited environments.
    *   Limited Multilingual Validation:  The paper acknowledges a reliance on English-language narratives, limiting the generalizability of findings across multilingual contexts, as well as lack of multimodal VA datasets.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of global health surveillance by demonstrating the feasibility of using LLMs to improve verbal autopsy accuracy. The LA-VA pipeline can serve as a foundation for future research and development of more robust and accessible COD prediction tools. The exploration of ensemble methods is a valuable contribution.
*   **Further Research:**
    *   Investigating open-source LLMs as substitutes for GPT-5 is crucial.
    *   Validating the pipeline on more recent and diverse datasets would increase confidence in its generalizability.
    *   Addressing computational and privacy concerns is essential for practical deployment in resource-limited settings.
    *   Exploring multimodal VA datasets and multilingual narratives would improve the robustness of the approach.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel application of LLMs to a significant problem in global health. The rigorous evaluation, use of a real-world dataset, and focus on low-resource settings are strengths. However, the reliance on a closed-source LLM is a major limitation. The significance of verbal autopsy improvements and the solid experimental results balances some of the limitations and places the paper in a good position. The limitations however call for more rigorous research in these directions.

### Mechanistic Learning with Guided Diffusion Models to Predict Spatio-Temporal Brain Tumor Growth
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09610v1)
- **Authors**: Daria Laslo, Efthymios Georgiou, Marius George Linguraru, Andreas Rauschecker, Sabine Muller, Catherine R. Jutzeler, Sarah Bruningk
- **Abstract**: Predicting the spatio-temporal progression of brain tumors is essential for guiding clinical decisions in neuro-oncology. We propose a hybrid mechanistic learning framework that combines a mathematical tumor growth model with a guided denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM) to synthesize anatomically feasible future MRIs from preceding scans. The mechanistic model, formulated as a system of ordinary differential equations, captures temporal tumor dynamics including radiotherapy effects and estimates future tumor burden. These estimates condition a gradient-guided DDIM, enabling image synthesis that aligns with both predicted growth and patient anatomy. We train our model on the BraTS adult and pediatric glioma datasets and evaluate on 60 axial slices of in-house longitudinal pediatric diffuse midline glioma (DMG) cases. Our framework generates realistic follow-up scans based on spatial similarity metrics. It also introduces tumor growth probability maps, which capture both clinically relevant extent and directionality of tumor growth as shown by 95th percentile Hausdorff Distance. The method enables biologically informed image generation in data-limited scenarios, offering generative-space-time predictions that account for mechanistic priors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a novel hybrid mechanistic learning framework for predicting spatio-temporal brain tumor growth, particularly in data-limited scenarios.  It combines a mechanistic ordinary differential equation (ODE) model of tumor dynamics (incorporating radiotherapy effects) with a guided denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM) for synthesizing realistic follow-up MRI scans.  The mechanistic model provides estimates of future tumor burden, which then conditions the DDIM to generate anatomically feasible images consistent with both predicted growth and individual patient anatomy.  The model is trained and evaluated on the BraTS dataset and longitudinal pediatric diffuse midline glioma (DMG) cases, demonstrating its ability to generate realistic follow-up scans and tumor growth probability maps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of a mechanistic model with a generative diffusion model for longitudinal tumor growth prediction.  While both mechanistic modeling of tumors and generative models for medical imaging exist, their combination for spatio-temporal *prediction* in a longitudinal setting, especially addressing data scarcity, is a significant advancement. Existing works focus on either spatial generation *or* temporal prediction but rarely both.  The use of the mechanistic model to *guide* the DDIM is also a valuable contribution, ensuring biologically plausible tumor evolution in the synthesized images. The paper leverages the strengths of both approaches; the ODE model captures temporal dynamics, while the DDIM handles complex spatial details and anatomical plausibility.

*   **Significance:**  Predicting tumor growth is crucial for clinical decision-making in neuro-oncology. This framework could potentially aid in:

    *   **Improved Treatment Planning:** By providing plausible future scenarios, it can assist in optimizing radiotherapy planning and predicting treatment response.
    *   **Anticipatory Symptom Management:** Predicting tumor progression enables proactive symptom management and improved patient care.
    *   **Data Augmentation:** Generating synthetic longitudinal data to address data scarcity issues for training other machine-learning models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Hybrid Approach:** Combining mechanistic and generative modeling provides both biological interpretability and realistic image synthesis.
    *   **Data-Efficiency:** Addresses the critical problem of limited longitudinal data in rare diseases like DMG.
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:**  Uses appropriate metrics like SSIM and Hausdorff Distance for image quality and prediction performance.
    *   **Clinical Relevance:** Demonstrates promising results in the context of pediatric brain tumors, a challenging area.
    *   **Tumor Growth Probability Maps:** Provides an intuitive visualization of potential tumor growth patterns.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **ODE Model Simplicity:** The ODE model, while incorporating key aspects of tumor dynamics and radiotherapy response, is a relatively simplified representation of complex biological processes. More sophisticated models might improve prediction accuracy.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** Mechanistic models can be sensitive to parameter selection, and accurate patient-specific parameter estimation can be difficult. The bootstrapping approach helps address this, but further investigation into parameter identifiability and uncertainty quantification is warranted.
    *   **Evaluation Dataset Size:** While the use of in-house DMG data is valuable, the relatively small size (n=8 patients, 60 slices) limits the generalizability of the findings. A larger, more diverse cohort would strengthen the results.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** Further ablation studies (e.g., comparing performance without the mechanistic guidance) would better quantify the contribution of each component of the framework.
    *   **Reliance on Manual Segmentations:** The evaluation relies on manual tumor segmentations, which are time-consuming and subject to inter-observer variability. Automating or semi-automating the segmentation process would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The framework has the potential to significantly impact the field of neuro-oncology by providing a powerful tool for predicting tumor growth and treatment response. It opens up new avenues for research in personalized medicine and AI-assisted clinical decision-making.

**Justification:**

The paper represents a solid contribution, combining existing methods in a novel way to tackle a clinically relevant problem with limited data. While there are some limitations, especially regarding the complexity of the mechanistic model and the size of the evaluation dataset, the initial results are promising and the concept is sound. The integration of mechanistic knowledge with generative AI is a promising direction for medical image analysis.
