### One Head, Many Models: Cross-Attention Routing for Cost-Aware LLM Selection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09782v1)
- **Authors**: Roshini Pulishetty, Mani Kishan Ghantasala, Keerthy Kaushik Dasoju, Niti Mangwani, Vishal Garimella, Aditya Mate, Somya Chatterjee, Yue Kang, Ehi Nosakhare, Sadid Hasan, Soundar Srinivasan
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of large language models (LLMs) with varying computational costs and performance profiles presents a critical challenge for scalable, cost-effective deployment in real-world applications. We introduce a unified routing framework that leverages a single-head cross-attention mechanism to jointly model query and model embeddings, enabling dynamic selection of the optimal LLM for each input query. Our approach is evaluated on RouterBench, a large-scale, publicly available benchmark encompassing diverse LLM pools and domains. By explicitly capturing fine-grained query-model interactions, our router predicts both response quality and generation cost, achieving up to 6.6% improvement in Average Improvement in Quality (AIQ) and 2.9% in maximum performance over existing routers. To robustly balance performance and cost, we propose an exponential reward function that enhances stability across user preferences. The resulting architecture is lightweight, generalizes effectively across domains, and demonstrates improved efficiency compared to prior methods, establishing a new standard for cost-aware LLM routing.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's analyze this paper on cross-attention routing for cost-aware LLM selection.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a unified LLM routing framework that employs a single-head cross-attention mechanism to dynamically select the most suitable LLM for a given input query, balancing response quality and computational cost. This framework learns to jointly model query and model embeddings, predicting both response quality and generation cost. The approach is evaluated on the RouterBench benchmark, demonstrating improved Average Improvement in Quality (AIQ) and maximum performance compared to existing routers. The paper also proposes an exponential reward function to enhance stability across varying user preferences. The resulting router is lightweight, generalizes across domains, and offers improved efficiency for cost-aware LLM routing.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper tackles a relevant and important problem in the field of LLM deployment: efficient and cost-effective model selection.  The core idea of using cross-attention to model query-model interactions is interesting and forms the basis for its potential novelty. Let's break down the strengths and weaknesses:

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Practical Problem:** The paper directly addresses the challenge of efficiently selecting from a pool of LLMs with varying cost-performance profiles, a crucial concern for real-world deployment, especially for hyperscalers.

*   **Cross-Attention for Query-Model Interaction:**  The use of cross-attention to explicitly model the interaction between the query and model embeddings is a key differentiator. This allows the router to potentially learn nuanced relationships and predict performance on a per-query basis, as opposed to relying solely on query features or model-agnostic heuristics.

*   **Unified Framework:**  The framework jointly models quality and cost, providing a more holistic view of the routing decision.

*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments on RouterBench, a recognized benchmark, provide evidence of the approach's effectiveness, demonstrating improvements in AIQ and maximum performance.
*   **Exponential reward function** introduces more stability across user preferences.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the use of cross-attention is a valuable addition, its novelty is *incremental*. Cross-attention is a well-established technique in NLP, and the paper's contribution primarily lies in *applying* it to the LLM routing problem rather than introducing a fundamentally new attention mechanism.
*   **Dependency on Pre-trained Embeddings:** The framework relies on pre-trained embeddings for both queries and models. While this simplifies the training process, it also limits the router's ability to learn representations tailored specifically for routing. The quality of these embeddings likely has a significant impact on the performance of the router.
*   **Limited Ablation Depth:**  While the paper presents ablation studies on predictor architectures, a deeper analysis of the various components of the cross-attention module (e.g., different types of attention, different ways of constructing model embeddings) would be beneficial.
*   **Comparative Baselines:**  While the paper compares against RouterBench baselines,  a more in-depth comparison with other recent LLM routing methods (e.g., Universal Model Routing or those cited in the related works that came online only a few days apart) would further strengthen the results. The paper mentions a future comparison with 'contemporary baselines' as future work.
*   **Limited Generalization Claims:**  The evaluation focuses on a single benchmark (RouterBench). While RouterBench is a valuable resource, expanding the evaluation to other datasets or LLM pools would improve the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Dependency on RouterBench dataset.** The paper mentions that a degree of uncertainty exists due to using same LLM responses in the RoutherBench dataset.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance is moderate. It offers a practical solution for a relevant problem and demonstrates empirical improvements over existing approaches. However, the incremental nature of the novelty and the limited scope of the evaluation temper the overall impact. The paper could potentially influence the development of more sophisticated LLM routing systems, particularly those that leverage query-model interactions.

**Overall Justification of Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and the relatively incremental nature of the novelty, I assign a score of **6**. The paper tackles a valuable problem, presents a reasonable solution, and supports its claims with empirical evidence. However, the limited novelty, dependency on pre-trained embeddings, limited ablation depth, and the scope of the evaluation hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. Additional experimentation and rigorous comparison with SOTA techniques will lead to a significantly more valuable contribution.

**Score: 6**

### How well can LLMs provide planning feedback in grounded environments?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09790v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxuan Li, Victor Zhong
- **Abstract**: Learning to plan in grounded environments typically requires carefully designed reward functions or high-quality annotated demonstrations. Recent works show that pretrained foundation models, such as large language models (LLMs) and vision language models (VLMs), capture background knowledge helpful for planning, which reduces the amount of reward design and demonstrations needed for policy learning. We evaluate how well LLMs and VLMs provide feedback across symbolic, language, and continuous control environments. We consider prominent types of feedback for planning including binary feedback, preference feedback, action advising, goal advising, and delta action feedback. We also consider inference methods that impact feedback performance, including in-context learning, chain-of-thought, and access to environment dynamics. We find that foundation models can provide diverse high-quality feedback across domains. Moreover, larger and reasoning models consistently provide more accurate feedback, exhibit less bias, and benefit more from enhanced inference methods. Finally, feedback quality degrades for environments with complex dynamics or continuous state spaces and action spaces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive study on the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to provide planning feedback in grounded environments.  It evaluates LLMs/VLMs as "Feedback Models" (FMs), comparing their feedback on agent behavior (e.g., whether an action is optimal, preference between actions, action advising) against ground truth derived from an optimal policy.  The study covers diverse environments (symbolic games, text environments, robot control), feedback types, inference methods (in-context learning, chain-of-thought), and models of varying size and reasoning capabilities. Key findings include that larger, reasoning-capable models provide higher-quality feedback, benefit more from advanced inference techniques, and are less biased.  Feedback quality degrades in complex dynamic or continuous action spaces. VLMs outperform LLMs in visual environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its broad and systematic evaluation of LLMs/VLMs as feedback providers for planning. While previous work has explored LLMs for specific feedback types or in limited domains, this study offers a more holistic view, comparing models across multiple dimensions. The evaluation of VLMs is a valuable addition.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for reinforcement learning and robotics. High-quality language model feedback could reduce the need for hand-engineered reward functions or extensive demonstrations, potentially simplifying the training of intelligent agents. Demonstrating the limitations of current models in complex environments helps direct future research efforts toward more robust and generalizable solutions. The work aligns with the growing trend of using LLMs to augment existing RL methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper examines a wide range of models, environments, feedback types, and inference methods, providing a rich dataset and a nuanced understanding of LLM/VLM feedback capabilities.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The comparison against ground truth derived from optimal policies provides a clear and objective measure of feedback quality.
    *   **Clear Findings:** The paper identifies several key factors that influence feedback performance, such as model size, reasoning ability, environment complexity, and observation modality.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The research highlights the potential of language model feedback for improving policy learning efficiency and effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:**  While the paper analyzes the *types* of errors (format, insufficient knowledge, etc.), a deeper dive into the *specific* scenarios where models fail and why could provide further insight.
    *   **Focus on Accuracy, Less on Efficiency:**  The paper primarily assesses feedback *accuracy*. An analysis of the *computational cost* of generating LLM/VLM feedback (especially with techniques like chain-of-thought) would be valuable.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Learning from Feedback:** The paper primarily focuses on *evaluating* feedback quality, not *using* the feedback to improve policy learning. While the "future work" section acknowledges this, a preliminary experiment in this direction could strengthen the paper.
    *  **In some of the domains with smaller dataset sizes it's still unclear if a large number of results is actually statistically significant**

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on language-guided reinforcement learning, robot learning, and the use of foundation models for decision-making. It provides a solid foundation for developing more effective methods for eliciting and utilizing language model feedback. It could spur the development of new techniques for improving LLM/VLM reasoning in grounded environments and for addressing the challenges posed by complex dynamics and continuous action spaces.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a systematic and comprehensive evaluation of LLMs/VLMs as feedback providers for planning. The findings are well-supported by the experimental results and have significant implications for future research. However, a deeper analysis of failure modes and a preliminary investigation into learning from the feedback could further enhance the paper's impact.

### HEFT: A Coarse-to-Fine Hierarchy for Enhancing the Efficiency and Accuracy of Language Model Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09801v1)
- **Authors**: Brennen Hill
- **Abstract**: The adaptation of large language models (LLMs) to specialized reasoning tasks is fundamentally constrained by computational resources. Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) methods have emerged as a powerful solution, yet the landscape of these techniques is diverse, with distinct methods operating in either the model's weight space or its representation space. This paper investigates the hypothesis that a synergistic combination of these paradigms can unlock superior performance and efficiency. We introduce HEFT (Hierarchical Efficient Fine-Tuning), a novel hierarchical adaptation strategy that composes two distinct PEFT methods in a coarse-to-fine manner: first, a broad, foundational adaptation in the weight space using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), followed by a precise, surgical refinement of internal activations using Representation Fine-Tuning (ReFT). We evaluate this approach by fine-tuning a Llama-2-7B model on the BoolQ benchmark, a challenging dataset for inferential reasoning. Our results reveal a profound synergistic effect. A model fine-tuned for only three epochs with our HEFT strategy achieves an accuracy of 85.17\%, exceeding the performance of models trained for 20 epochs with either LoRA-only (85.05\%) or ReFT-only (83.36\%) methodologies. This work demonstrates that the thoughtful composition of PEFT methods is a potent algorithmic innovation, offering a more efficient and effective path toward advancing the reasoning capabilities of language models. By achieving superior results with a fraction of the computational budget, our findings present a principled approach to overcoming the obstacles inherent in adapting large-scale models for complex cognitive tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HEFT (Hierarchical Efficient Fine-Tuning), a novel strategy for adapting large language models (LLMs) to specialized reasoning tasks. HEFT combines LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation), a weight-space adaptation method, with ReFT (Representation Fine-Tuning), which operates in the model's representation space. The process is structured hierarchically: LoRA first performs a broad, foundational tuning, followed by a more precise refinement of internal activations using ReFT. Experiments using a Llama-2-7B model on the BoolQ benchmark demonstrate that HEFT achieves higher accuracy with significantly less training compared to using either LoRA or ReFT alone.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining weight-space and representation-space adaptation methods in a hierarchical (coarse-to-fine) manner is novel. While the individual components, LoRA and ReFT, are known, their specific combination and hierarchical application are new. The paper demonstrates empirically that this composition yields synergistic performance improvements. However, the idea of combining PEFT methods has been explored. Thus, the novelty is incremental and rests on the specific combination and hierarchy rather than a radically new fundamental concept.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in LLM adaptation: the trade-off between performance and computational cost. By demonstrating that HEFT can achieve superior results with a fraction of the computational budget compared to single-method approaches or larger models, it offers a practical and efficient path to improving LLM reasoning capabilities. The success on BoolQ, a benchmark designed to evaluate inferential reasoning, further strengthens the significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-defined approach.
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of HEFT.
    *   Ablation studies comparing HEFT to single-method baselines showcase the synergistic effect.
    *   Comparison against zero-shot performance of larger models highlights the potential of HEFT.
    *   The paper clearly articulates the benefits in terms of both accuracy and computational efficiency.
    *   The "coarse-to-fine" rationale is well-justified.
    *   Provides implementation details allowing for reproducibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is limited to a single dataset (BoolQ), making it difficult to assess the generalizability of HEFT to other tasks and domains.
    *   The study only explores one hierarchical order (LoRA then ReFT), without investigating other possibilities (e.g., ReFT then LoRA, interleaved application).
    *   Hyperparameter tuning is limited. The authors acknowledge that further optimization could lead to even better results.
    *   The analysis is somewhat limited in providing a detailed mechanistic explanation of *why* the synergy occurs beyond the high-level "coarse-to-fine" rationale. While model modularity and curriculum learning are discussed, a deeper dive would be helpful.
    *   While the work discusses the combination of PEFT methods, it falls into the larger discussion of combining modular networks, which has been explored in other settings.
*   **Potential Influence:** HEFT offers a potentially valuable approach for researchers and practitioners working on LLM adaptation. The idea of combining different PEFT methods in a hierarchical way could inspire new research directions and lead to more efficient and effective LLM training strategies. The concept of "PEFT algebra" is also intriguing. The paper provides a concrete implementation that can be readily adopted and extended.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a clear, well-executed, and empirically supported method for improving the efficiency and accuracy of LLM adaptation. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the hierarchical composition and demonstration of synergistic benefits are significant. The limitations (single dataset, fixed order, limited hyperparameter tuning) detract from the overall score. The paper is a solid contribution to the field, demonstrating a practical and efficient approach to LLM adaptation, even though the novelty is more incremental than transformative.

### Towards a Common Framework for Autoformalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09810v1)
- **Authors**: Agnieszka Mensfelt, David Tena Cucala, Santiago Franco, Angeliki Koutsoukou-Argyraki, Vince Trencsenyi, Kostas Stathis
- **Abstract**: Autoformalization has emerged as a term referring to the automation of formalization - specifically, the formalization of mathematics using interactive theorem provers (proof assistants). Its rapid development has been driven by progress in deep learning, especially large language models (LLMs). More recently, the term has expanded beyond mathematics to describe the broader task of translating informal input into formal logical representations. At the same time, a growing body of research explores using LLMs to translate informal language into formal representations for reasoning, planning, and knowledge representation - often without explicitly referring to this process as autoformalization. As a result, despite addressing similar tasks, the largely independent development of these research areas has limited opportunities for shared methodologies, benchmarks, and theoretical frameworks that could accelerate progress. The goal of this paper is to review - explicit or implicit - instances of what can be considered autoformalization and to propose a unified framework, encouraging cross-pollination between different fields to advance the development of next generation AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the growing field of "autoformalization," which involves automatically translating informal language (e.g., natural language or semi-formal mathematical notation) into formal languages suitable for logical reasoning and automated verification. The authors observe that research in this area is fragmented across different subfields (mathematics formalization, logical inference, planning, knowledge representation), each with its own terminology and methodologies. To address this, the paper proposes a unified framework for understanding autoformalization. This framework comprises four elements: informal language (Li), a domain-specific subset of Li, formal language (Lf), and a semantic equivalence criterion (E). The paper reviews existing literature, categorizes papers into these subfields, and illustrates how the proposed framework can capture different autoformalization tasks through case studies. The goal is to encourage cross-pollination and accelerate progress in the development of next-generation AI systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its attempt to unify the diverse landscape of autoformalization research under a single, well-defined framework. While individual components of the framework (e.g., defining semantic equivalence) are not entirely new, their synthesis into a comprehensive model for describing and comparing different autoformalization approaches is a valuable contribution. Previous works often focus on specific tasks and formalisms, lacking a high-level perspective. The observation that different communities address similar challenges with limited cross-communication is insightful.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its potential to promote collaboration and knowledge sharing across subfields. By providing a common language and framework, researchers can more easily compare different approaches, identify shared challenges, and leverage each other's advances. This unification could lead to the development of more general and robust autoformalization tools, which have the potential to improve reasoning, verification, and safety in various AI applications. The explicit mention of the connection to LLMs and the potential for better "reasoning" by leveraging them is a significant aspect. The discussion of the limitations of solely relying on LLMs and the potential of combining them with formal reasoning tools highlights an important direction.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Definition:** The paper provides a relatively clear and understandable definition of autoformalization and its key components.
*   **Comprehensive Review:** The literature review, while not exhaustive, provides a good overview of the diverse range of research activities relevant to autoformalization.
*   **Case Studies:** The case studies effectively illustrate how the framework can be applied to different tasks and formalisms.
*   **Identifies Key Challenges:** The paper highlights important challenges such as semantic verification, target language selection, and scalability.
*   **Promotes Collaboration:** The paper explicitly aims to foster interdisciplinary collaboration.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Formalism:** While the framework provides a conceptual structure, it lacks a more rigorous mathematical formalization. Defining each element precisely would make the framework more powerful.
*   **Subjectivity of Semantic Equivalence:** The concept of semantic equivalence, and especially the distinction between E and V, is inherently subjective and context-dependent. More discussion is needed on practical strategies for specifying and validating semantic equivalence in different settings.
*   **Uneven Depth:** The paper is somewhat uneven in its depth of coverage. Some sections are more detailed and insightful than others. The "interactive formalization" discussion is a good point but feels somewhat underdeveloped.
*   **Validation of the Framework:** The paper presents the framework and illustrates its use, but it does not provide empirical evidence or comparative analysis to demonstrate its benefits over existing approaches.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a common ground for researchers and practitioners. However, its actual impact will depend on whether the proposed framework is adopted and used as a basis for future research and development. Its influence is increased if it promotes better LLM-based reasoning or more rigorous AI development in general.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by unifying a fragmented research area and highlighting key challenges. The framework is reasonably well-defined and illustrated through case studies. However, the lack of a more rigorous formalization, the inherent subjectivity of semantic equivalence, and the lack of empirical validation limit the paper's overall impact. Despite these limitations, the paper's potential to foster collaboration and guide future research justifies a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards an AI-based knowledge assistant for goat farmers based on Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09848v1)
- **Authors**: Nana Han, Dong Liu, Tomas Norton
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being recognised as valuable knowledge communication tools in many industries. However, their application in livestock farming remains limited, being constrained by several factors not least the availability, diversity and complexity of knowledge sources. This study introduces an intelligent knowledge assistant system designed to support health management in farmed goats. Leveraging the Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), two structured knowledge processing methods, table textualization and decision-tree textualization, were proposed to enhance large language models' (LLMs) understanding of heterogeneous data formats. Based on these methods, a domain-specific goat farming knowledge base was established to improve LLM's capacity for cross-scenario generalization. The knowledge base spans five key domains: Disease Prevention and Treatment, Nutrition Management, Rearing Management, Goat Milk Management, and Basic Farming Knowledge. Additionally, an online search module is integrated to enable real-time retrieval of up-to-date information. To evaluate system performance, six ablation experiments were conducted to examine the contribution of each component. The results demonstrated that heterogeneous knowledge fusion method achieved the best results, with mean accuracies of 87.90% on the validation set and 84.22% on the test set. Across the text-based, table-based, decision-tree based Q&A tasks, accuracy consistently exceeded 85%, validating the effectiveness of structured knowledge fusion within a modular design. Error analysis identified omission as the predominant error category, highlighting opportunities to further improve retrieval coverage and context integration. In conclusion, the results highlight the robustness and reliability of the proposed system for practical applications in goat farming.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an AI-based knowledge assistant for goat farmers, leveraging Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). It proposes methods for structuring and converting tabular data and decision trees into text to enhance LLM understanding of heterogeneous farming knowledge. A domain-specific knowledge base is created, combined with a real-time online retrieval module. The system's performance is evaluated through ablation experiments, demonstrating improved accuracy with the heterogeneous knowledge fusion method. The system is robust and reliable, potentially applicable in goat farming practices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Application:** The paper addresses a real-world problem with a potentially impactful solution for goat farmers.
    *   **Knowledge Structure:** The approach of structuring and textualizing tabular and decision-tree data is relevant to many domains where knowledge exists in diverse formats.
    *   **RAG Implementation:** The application of RAG is well-motivated, grounding LLM responses in verified domain-specific sources.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The use of ablation experiments provides a structured method of measuring the individual contributions of each system component.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis provides insights for optimizing the system by highlighting common error types and suggesting targeted areas for improvement (e.g., better retrieval and external information filtering).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Knowledge Base Size:** The dataset of 125 goat farming articles is relatively small. This could affect the generalizability of the findings, especially as the model's success relies heavily on the availability of relevant knowledge.
    *   **Online Search Refinement:** The study acknowledges that filtering and integrating information from the web search module requires further refinement. Current external resource integration appears to have a limited positive impact.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Data:** There is a limited number of test examples, especially of novel or emerging queries requiring real-time external information, which affects the statistical significance of the evaluation. The number of examples in validation and test data (86 and 180) are considered small for a task of this complexity.
    *   **Dependence on BERTScore:** The evaluation heavily relies on BERTScore. While this metric offers advantages over traditional n-gram-based metrics, it's still a proxy for actual usefulness and correctness. More detailed examination of the response quality is important to reveal the problems beyond BERTSCORE-F1 threshold.

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using RAG in an agricultural context for goat farming is relatively novel. The structure of this paper has several novelties: it incorporates a pre-trained multilingual embedding model BGE-M3, a hybrid retrieval mechanism using a domain-specific knowledge base, Google Search API and a modular prompt engineering strategy. But the specific components (table-to-text conversion, use of RAG) have been explored in other domains. The novelty mainly lies in the combination and adaptation for this specific application and domain.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to translate AI technologies into tangible benefits for a specific agricultural community. Demonstrating the feasibility of knowledge-augmented LLMs in this context could inspire similar developments in other niche domains with limited digital resources. It points to a future where domain experts can build intelligent systems more effectively without needing extensive software engineering skills.

**Justification of Score:**

This paper exhibits promising results and addresses a practical need. However, the limitations surrounding the knowledge base size and the need for better web content integration temper its significance. Additionally, while the combination of existing techniques is well-executed, the core components aren't groundbreaking in isolation. The evaluation is good, with its methodical approach to ablation, but could benefit from being more expansive in the data samples and evaluation metrics used.
