- **Score**: 6/10

### Topic-Guided Reinforcement Learning with LLMs for Enhancing Multi-Document Summarization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09852v1)
- **Authors**: Chuyuan Li, Austin Xu, Shafiq Joty, Giuseppe Carenini
- **Abstract**: A key challenge in Multi-Document Summarization (MDS) is effectively integrating information from multiple sources while maintaining coherence and topical relevance. While Large Language Models have shown impressive results in single-document summarization, their performance on MDS still leaves room for improvement. In this paper, we propose a topic-guided reinforcement learning approach to improve content selection in MDS. We first show that explicitly prompting models with topic labels enhances the informativeness of the generated summaries. Building on this insight, we propose a novel topic reward within the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) framework to measure topic alignment between the generated summary and source documents. Experimental results on the Multi-News and Multi-XScience datasets demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms strong baselines, highlighting the effectiveness of leveraging topical cues in MDS.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, assigning a score from 1 to 10 with a thorough justification.

**Paper Summary:**

The paper "Topic-Guided Reinforcement Learning with LLMs for Enhancing Multi-Document Summarization" introduces a novel approach to multi-document summarization (MDS) by leveraging explicit topic guidance within a reinforcement learning (RL) framework. The key idea is that by explicitly guiding the summarization process with topic labels, the model can more effectively integrate information from multiple sources while maintaining coherence and topical relevance. The authors first demonstrate that prompting Large Language Models (LLMs) with topic labels improves the informativeness of generated summaries. Building on this, they propose a novel "topic reward" function that measures the alignment between the topics in a generated summary and the topics present in the source documents. This reward function is then integrated into the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) framework, enabling RL training to optimize for topic alignment.  The method is evaluated on the Multi-News and Multi-XScience datasets, showing improvements over strong baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of techniques:
    *   **Explicit Topic Guidance:** While previous work has explored topic modeling for summarization, this paper directly uses explicit topic labels as prompts and within the RL reward function. This is more direct and interpretable than latent topic approaches.
    *   **Topic-F1 Reward:** The proposed topic reward function, which leverages a harmonic mean of coverage and precision, is a novel way to quantify the semantic similarity between summaries and source documents with respect to topic alignment.
    *   **Integration with GRPO:** The integration of this topic reward into the GRPO framework for reinforcement learning is a relatively new application of this RL optimization technique in the context of MDS.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a well-known challenge in MDS: maintaining coherence and topical relevance when integrating information from multiple sources. The demonstrated improvements over strong baselines on standard datasets indicate that the proposed approach can be effective in enhancing the quality of MDS systems. The use of smaller, fine-tuned LLMs, guided by larger LLMs for topic extraction, also mirrors knowledge distillation, highlighting practical applicability. The ablation studies analyzing component contributions are also helpful. However, I note the following:

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The approach heavily relies on the quality of LLMs for both topic extraction and summary generation. The reported results are tied to the specific models (Qwen series) used in the experiments.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Metrics:** While the paper uses standard metrics such as ROUGE, LLM2Vec, and BERTScore, as well as their own alignment scores, more human evaluation could further strengthen the significance of the findings.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The gains over some baselines, while consistent, might be considered incremental in some aspects, particularly on Multi-News compared to Multi-XScience.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation of the methodology.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation with ablation studies.
    *   Novel combination of topic guidance and reinforcement learning for MDS.
    *   The approach is relatively interpretable due to the explicit use of topic labels.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Strong dependence on LLM performance.
    *   Potentially incremental improvements in some cases.
    *   The results are dependent on hyperparameters tuned for the selected models and datasets and may not generalize to other models or datasets. More analysis on parameter sensitivity could enhance value.

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to MDS, combining explicit topic guidance with reinforcement learning. While the reliance on LLMs and the incremental nature of some improvements are limitations, the paper addresses an important challenge in MDS and provides a promising direction for future research. The topic F1 reward and combination of knowledge distillation with GRPO are useful and interesting.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SWE-Effi: Re-Evaluating Software AI Agent System Effectiveness Under Resource Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09853v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyu Fan, Kirill Vasilevski, Dayi Lin, Boyuan Chen, Yihao Chen, Zhiqing Zhong, Jie M. Zhang, Pinjia He, Ahmed E. Hassan
- **Abstract**: The advancement of large language models (LLMs) and code agents has demonstrated significant potential to assist software engineering (SWE) tasks, such as autonomous issue resolution and feature addition. Existing AI for software engineering leaderboards (e.g., SWE-bench) focus solely on solution accuracy, ignoring the crucial factor of effectiveness in a resource-constrained world. This is a universal problem that also exists beyond software engineering tasks: any AI system should be more than correct - it must also be cost-effective. To address this gap, we introduce SWE-Effi, a set of new metrics to re-evaluate AI systems in terms of holistic effectiveness scores. We define effectiveness as the balance between the accuracy of outcome (e.g., issue resolve rate) and the resources consumed (e.g., token and time). In this paper, we specifically focus on the software engineering scenario by re-ranking popular AI systems for issue resolution on a subset of the SWE-bench benchmark using our new multi-dimensional metrics. We found that AI system's effectiveness depends not just on the scaffold itself, but on how well it integrates with the base model, which is key to achieving strong performance in a resource-efficient manner. We also identified systematic challenges such as the "token snowball" effect and, more significantly, a pattern of "expensive failures". In these cases, agents consume excessive resources while stuck on unsolvable tasks - an issue that not only limits practical deployment but also drives up the cost of failed rollouts during RL training. Lastly, we observed a clear trade-off between effectiveness under the token budget and effectiveness under the time budget, which plays a crucial role in managing project budgets and enabling scalable reinforcement learning, where fast responses are essential.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SWE-Effi, a new set of metrics for evaluating the effectiveness of AI systems in software engineering tasks, specifically issue resolution, under resource constraints (token and time budgets).  Current benchmarks like SWE-bench primarily focus on accuracy (resolve rate) without considering the cost associated with achieving that accuracy.  SWE-Effi aims to address this gap by re-evaluating AI systems using metrics that balance accuracy with resource consumption. The authors re-rank popular AI systems for issue resolution on a subset of SWE-bench using their new multi-dimensional metrics and identify key findings: (1) an AI system's effectiveness is heavily dependent on the synergy between the scaffold and the base LLM; (2) the "token snowball" effect, where each API call adds unnecessary context, escalating resource consumption; and (3) the issue of "expensive failures," where agents consume excessive resources while stuck on unsolvable tasks.  Finally, the paper shows a clear trade-off between performance under different budget conditions (token/time).

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in highlighting the importance of resource efficiency in AI-driven software engineering and proposing a multi-dimensional evaluation framework (SWE-Effi) to address this issue. While the individual components (metrics like resolve rate, CPU time, token usage) are not new, the *combination* and *focus* on resource efficiency as a key performance indicator provide a novel perspective. The concept of "expensive failures" is a particularly interesting observation and a useful way to describe a crucial problem. The framework also provides a structured way to evaluate systems in resource-constrained environments, which is relevant and important.

**Significance:** The work is significant because it directly addresses a real-world limitation of current AI-driven SWE systems.  Ignoring resource constraints can lead to impractical or unsustainable solutions, hindering real-world deployment and efficient RL training. By emphasizing resource efficiency, SWE-Effi encourages the development of more practical and scalable AI tools for software engineering. Identifying systematic issues like "expensive failures" and "token snowball" provides actionable insights for researchers and developers aiming to improve the efficiency of these systems. The emphasis on the need for LLM-scaffold synergy is also important and provides a path for developers to follow.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clearly defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the gap in current evaluation metrics for AI-driven SWE systems.
*   **Well-defined metrics:**  SWE-Effi offers a structured and measurable approach to evaluating resource efficiency.
*   **Actionable insights:** The identified issues (expensive failures, token snowball, LLM-scaffold synergy) provide specific directions for future research and development.
*   **Solid methodology:**  The paper uses a standard benchmark (SWE-bench) and established techniques (AUC) to evaluate the proposed metrics.
*   **Empirical evidence:** The results from re-evaluating existing systems provide concrete examples of the importance of resource efficiency.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is performed on a limited subset of SWE-bench and a relatively small selection of scaffolds and LLMs, which could affect the generalizability of the findings. The authors acknowledge these limitations, but expanding the evaluation would strengthen the results.
*   **Simple metrics:** While valuable, the metrics themselves (CPU time, token usage) are quite basic. Exploring more sophisticated metrics related to code quality (e.g., security vulnerabilities introduced by the fix) under resource constraints would add depth.
*   **Over-simplification of cost:** The monetary cost model (using USD per token) might not fully capture the complexity of real-world costs associated with using LLMs in practice, such as potential service level agreement (SLA) issues or other contractual costs.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7/10**.

**Rationale:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by shifting the focus of evaluation in AI-driven SWE from solely accuracy to a balance between accuracy and resource efficiency. It introduces SWE-Effi, a useful framework for evaluating systems under resource constraints. The identified issues, particularly "expensive failures" and "token snowball" offer valuable insights that have implications to the broader research community. The emphasis on LLM-scaffold synergy is a good reminder to developers to follow this path.

However, the limited scope of the evaluation and relatively basic metrics prevent a higher score. More comprehensive analysis with a larger set of LLMs, benchmarks, and a finer-grained approach to cost metrics would give the paper more impact. It will be important to follow this study with more comprehensive evaluations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Vibe Check: Understanding the Effects of LLM-Based Conversational Agents' Personality and Alignment on User Perceptions in Goal-Oriented Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09870v1)
- **Authors**: Hasibur Rahman, Smit Desai
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) enable conversational agents (CAs) to express distinctive personalities, raising new questions about how such designs shape user perceptions. This study investigates how personality expression levels and user-agent personality alignment influence perceptions in goal-oriented tasks. In a between-subjects experiment (N=150), participants completed travel planning with CAs exhibiting low, medium, or high expression across the Big Five traits, controlled via our novel Trait Modulation Keys framework. Results revealed an inverted-U relationship: medium expression produced the most positive evaluations across Intelligence, Enjoyment, Anthropomorphism, Intention to Adopt, Trust, and Likeability, significantly outperforming both extremes. Personality alignment further enhanced outcomes, with Extraversion and Emotional Stability emerging as the most influential traits. Cluster analysis identified three distinct compatibility profiles, with "Well-Aligned" users reporting substantially positive perceptions. These findings demonstrate that personality expression and strategic trait alignment constitute optimal design targets for CA personality, offering design implications as LLM-based CAs become increasingly prevalent.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Vibe Check: Understanding the Effects of LLM-Based Conversational Agents' Personality and Alignment on User Perceptions in Goal-Oriented Tasks" investigates how personality expression levels and user-agent personality alignment affect user perceptions of Large Language Model (LLM)-based conversational agents (CAs) in goal-oriented tasks. The authors introduce a novel prompting framework called Trait Modulation Keys (TMK) to control Big Five personality traits at different expression levels (low, medium, high) without relying on biographical backstories. Through a user study (N=150) where participants interacted with CAs for travel planning, the results showed an inverted-U relationship: medium expression resulted in the most positive user evaluations (Intelligence, Enjoyment, Anthropomorphism, Intention to Adopt, Trust, Likeability). Moreover, personality alignment further enhanced positive outcomes, with Extraversion and Emotional Stability being key factors. The authors identified distinct user compatibility profiles and offer design implications for personality-aware AI systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel elements:
    *   **TMK Prompting Framework:**  The TMK framework is a useful contribution. It provides a structured method to control multiple personality traits concurrently and at varying intensities, overcoming the limitations of previous binary or extreme-based manipulations and biographical backstories. The TMK validation provides an objective methodology for evaluating LLM prompting that has been absent in the literature.
    *   **Medium Personality Expression:** The exploration of medium-level personality expression is novel. Previous studies often focused on extremes, leaving a gap in understanding nuanced personality configurations.
    *   **Alignment with multi-dimensional personalities:** Most research focuses on overall persona alignment, not the individual traits.

*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications for the design of CAs:
    *   The inverted-U relationship suggests that a moderate personality is better than extreme ones, a valuable insight for designers.
    *   Identifying Extraversion and Emotional Stability as significant traits for alignment offers actionable design guidance.
    *   Reconceptualizing personality alignment beyond simple binary matches opens up avenues for more sophisticated CA design strategies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study employs a controlled experimental design with a reasonable sample size.
    *   **Thorough Validation:** Extensive validation of the TMK prompting framework demonstrated its reliability and steerability.
    *   **Clear Results:** The findings are clearly presented and supported by statistical analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Task Domain:** The travel planning scenario, while convenient, might limit the generalizability of the findings. Personality preferences may vary across different task domains (e.g., healthcare, education).
    *   **Cultural Sample:** The sample is limited to US adults and native English speakers, potentially affecting the universality of the results.
    *   **Static Personality:** The personalities were uniform across the entire interaction. A more complex approach would have been to introduce different types of personality dynamics.
    *   **Dependence on specific LLM:** The CA was built with GPT-40 which is specific and changes, so generalizability of results to current LLMs is an open question.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The study has the potential to influence the design of CAs by providing empirical evidence for the benefits of medium personality expression and the importance of considering user-agent personality alignment.
    *   The TMK framework can be used as a tool by other researchers and practitioners to create and evaluate personality-aware CAs.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper exhibits strong methodological rigor and actionable insights, it suffers slightly from limitations related to task domain, cultural sample, dependence on specific LLM and potential generalizability to the evolving LLM space. The contribution is valuable but not transformative, given the narrow focus on a specific task and population. The exploration of medium-level personality, and multi-dimensional personality traits makes it a compelling work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Emulating Public Opinion: A Proof-of-Concept of AI-Generated Synthetic Survey Responses for the Chilean Case
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09871v1)
- **Authors**: Bastián González-Bustamante, Nando Verelst, Carla Cisternas
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promising avenues for methodological and applied innovations in survey research by using synthetic respondents to emulate human answers and behaviour, potentially mitigating measurement and representation errors. However, the extent to which LLMs recover aggregate item distributions remains uncertain and downstream applications risk reproducing social stereotypes and biases inherited from training data. We evaluate the reliability of LLM-generated synthetic survey responses against ground-truth human responses from a Chilean public opinion probabilistic survey. Specifically, we benchmark 128 prompt-model-question triplets, generating 189,696 synthetic profiles, and pool performance metrics (i.e., accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score) in a meta-analysis across 128 question-subsample pairs to test for biases along key sociodemographic dimensions. The evaluation spans OpenAI's GPT family and o-series reasoning models, as well as Llama and Qwen checkpoints. Three results stand out. First, synthetic responses achieve excellent performance on trust items (F1-score and accuracy > 0.90). Second, GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini and Llama 4 Maverick perform comparably on this task. Third, synthetic-human alignment is highest among respondents aged 45-59. Overall, LLM-based synthetic samples approximate responses from a probabilistic sample, though with substantial item-level heterogeneity. Capturing the full nuance of public opinion remains challenging and requires careful calibration and additional distributional tests to ensure algorithmic fidelity and reduce errors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate synthetic survey responses in the context of Chilean public opinion. It benchmarks several state-of-the-art LLMs (OpenAI's GPT family, Llama, Qwen, and Gemma) against a ground-truth probabilistic survey conducted by the Centro de Estudios Públicos (CEP) in Chile. The research compares the performance of synthetic responses against real human responses, focusing on accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score. The authors also conduct a meta-analysis to identify potential biases along sociodemographic dimensions (age, gender, area). They test different prompting strategies, including using only demographic information versus incorporating attitudinal data. The key findings are that LLMs perform well on trust-related questions, that GPT-4o variants and Llama 4 Maverick perform similarly, and that the synthetic-human alignment is strongest in the 45-59 age group. The paper concludes that while LLMs can approximate survey responses, substantial item-level heterogeneity and the need for careful calibration remain challenges.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The idea of using LLMs to generate synthetic survey data isn't entirely new, as acknowledged by the authors. However, the paper contributes by providing a rigorous empirical benchmark of various LLMs against a real-world public opinion survey in a specific geographic and cultural context (Chile).  The meta-analysis of bias using sociodemographic subgroups is also a valuable addition. While not groundbreaking, the focus on algorithmic fidelity and identifying subgroup distortions pushes the field forward.

* **Significance:** The potential significance of this work lies in its implications for survey research.  If LLMs can generate reliable synthetic data, it could significantly reduce the cost and burden of traditional surveys, enable rapid data collection, facilitate experimentation on sensitive topics, and address limitations like non-response bias.  However, the paper's findings also highlight the risks of bias and the need for careful validation.  The emphasis on distribution similarity and bias detection is crucial for advancing the field responsibly.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined methodology, including the use of established evaluation metrics, different prompting strategies, and a meta-analysis of bias.
    * **Real-World Data:** Using a probabilistic survey conducted by a reputable organization (CEP) as ground-truth strengthens the validity of the findings.
    * **Benchmarking:** Comparing multiple LLMs provides valuable insights into their relative performance.
    * **Bias Detection:** The meta-analysis of sociodemographic subgroups is essential for identifying potential biases in synthetic data.
    * **Clear Limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their work, including the lack of testing for educational or socioeconomic bias, and the challenges in capturing the nuanced complexity of public opinion.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on a single country (Chile) and a relatively small number of questions, which limits the generalizability of the findings.
    * **Lack of In-depth Distributional Analysis:** While the authors mention the need for distributional tests, the paper lacks a detailed analysis of distributional similarity using metrics like Earth Mover's Distance (EMD). The paper only focused on the F1-score.
    * **Prompting Strategy Limitation:** The prompting strategy only focused on demographic and attitudinal dimensions, it could be worth to explore prompting the LLMs with other background information, such as a specific event or news item.

* **Overall Impact:** The paper provides a valuable empirical contribution to the growing body of research on synthetic survey data using LLMs.  It highlights both the potential and the limitations of this approach, emphasizing the importance of rigorous validation and bias detection. The findings are relevant to researchers and practitioners in survey research, political science, and computational social science.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has a mix of strengths and weaknesses. It is not a groundbreaking theoretical contribution, but it offers a strong empirical evaluation of LLMs for generating survey data in a specific context. While some limitations regarding the scope and type of questions are present, it moves the field forward by contributing a solid evaluation and bias detection analysis. Thus, it is above average, but not in the top tier of impactful research.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Tackling One Health Risks: How Large Language Models are leveraged for Risk Negotiation and Consensus-building
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09906v1)
- **Authors**: Alexandra Fetsch, Iurii Savvateev, Racem Ben Romdhane, Martin Wiedmann, Artemiy Dimov, Maciej Durkalec, Josef Teichmann, Jakob Zinsstag, Konstantinos Koutsoumanis, Andreja Rajkovic, Jason Mann, Mauro Tonolla, Monika Ehling-Schulz, Matthias Filter, Sophia Johler
- **Abstract**: Key global challenges of our times are characterized by complex interdependencies and can only be effectively addressed through an integrated, participatory effort. Conventional risk analysis frameworks often reduce complexity to ensure manageability, creating silos that hinder comprehensive solutions. A fundamental shift towards holistic strategies is essential to enable effective negotiations between different sectors and to balance the competing interests of stakeholders. However, achieving this balance is often hindered by limited time, vast amounts of information, and the complexity of integrating diverse perspectives. This study presents an AI-assisted negotiation framework that incorporates large language models (LLMs) and AI-based autonomous agents into a negotiation-centered risk analysis workflow. The framework enables stakeholders to simulate negotiations, systematically model dynamics, anticipate compromises, and evaluate solution impacts. By leveraging LLMs' semantic analysis capabilities we could mitigate information overload and augment decision-making process under time constraints. Proof-of-concept implementations were conducted in two real-world scenarios: (i) prudent use of a biopesticide, and (ii) targeted wild animal population control. Our work demonstrates the potential of AI-assisted negotiation to address the current lack of tools for cross-sectoral engagement. Importantly, the solution's open source, web based design, suits for application by a broader audience with limited resources and enables users to tailor and develop it for their own needs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to facilitate risk negotiation and consensus-building in One Health contexts. It proposes an AI-assisted negotiation framework that integrates LLMs and AI-based autonomous agents into a negotiation-centered risk analysis workflow. The framework allows stakeholders to simulate negotiations, systematically model dynamics, anticipate compromises, and evaluate solution impacts. The authors demonstrate the framework's applicability through two real-world case scenarios: the use of a biopesticide ( *B. thuringiensis*) and targeted wild animal population control. The results indicate that LLMs can help mitigate information overload, augment the decision-making process under time constraints, and address the current lack of tools for cross-sectoral engagement in risk management. The authors emphasize the importance of a human-in-the-loop (HIL) approach to mitigate LLM limitations and potential biases and provide details about the implementation of the framework including templates and code access.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents an interesting application of LLMs to a challenging problem: multi-stakeholder risk negotiation. The core idea of using AI to facilitate discussions, model compromises, and streamline the decision-making process in One Health is promising. However, a critical evaluation reveals areas of strength and weakness:

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the *specific application* of LLMs and agent-based modeling to the *structured problem* of risk negotiation in the One Health domain. Using LLMs in risk assessment is not completely novel; the focus on *negotiation* as a key element distinguishes it.
*   **Significance:** The potential impact is significant if the framework can demonstrably improve the efficiency and effectiveness of risk management decisions across diverse sectors. Addressing a major challenge in One Health, a field characterized by complex trade-offs, provides considerable significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The paper provides clear, structured steps for implementation, complete with code access and templates, making the framework usable by others.
    *   **Real-World Scenarios:** The use of two distinct case studies strengthens the credibility of the findings and demonstrates the framework's adaptability.
    *   **Human-in-the-Loop:** Acknowledging the limitations of LLMs and emphasizing the importance of human oversight is crucial for responsible AI implementation.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Providing access to code, templates, and process details enhances the reproducibility of results.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Limitations:** While acknowledged, a deeper discussion of the LLM's limitations (hallucinations, biases, reasoning abilities) in the *specific context* of risk negotiation would be beneficial. How those risks are identified and mitigated in practice is not described in enough detail, although it was specified that it involves humans in the loop.
    *   **Generalizability:** The two case scenarios, while relevant, may not be representative of all One Health risk negotiation contexts. Further validation across diverse settings is needed.
    *   **Quantifiable Improvements:** The paper does not provide clear quantifiable metrics demonstrating the *improvement* achieved by using the LLM-assisted framework compared to traditional negotiation methods. This makes it hard to objectively ascertain the degree of improvement. The subjective feedback by stakeholders is not rigorous enough.
    *   **Lack of benchmark:** The paper fails to assess the framework's performance against a relevant baseline (e.g., traditional negotiation methods).

**Justification:**

While the paper presents a promising approach, several weaknesses limit its overall impact and significance. The lack of empirical evidence demonstrating quantifiable improvements over existing methods, the shallow analysis of LLM limitations in this context, and the limited assessment of generalizability are major drawbacks.

Score: 6

**Rigorous Rationale:**

A score of 6 reflects the fact that the paper *is* a valuable contribution to the area as a method with a comprehensive implementation for risk mitigation using LLM agents but it has limitations. The comprehensive nature of the methodology with its emphasis on reproducibility and detailed procedures including real world demonstrations adds great value. The framework has clear potential and a structured implementation approach, with a emphasis on human in the loop approaches, which raises the paper beyond a score of 5. However, the paper does not go above a 6 because while important, the discussion on LLM limitations is relatively shallow and the paper does not provide strong support for a quantifiable improvement with this method. This is a framework that is in the middle of the road to being highly influential but would require more validation to receive a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.MA
- **Score**: 6/10

### When Your Reviewer is an LLM: Biases, Divergence, and Prompt Injection Risks in Peer Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09912v1)
- **Authors**: Changjia Zhu, Junjie Xiong, Renkai Ma, Zhicong Lu, Yao Liu, Lingyao Li
- **Abstract**: Peer review is the cornerstone of academic publishing, yet the process is increasingly strained by rising submission volumes, reviewer overload, and expertise mismatches. Large language models (LLMs) are now being used as "reviewer aids," raising concerns about their fairness, consistency, and robustness against indirect prompt injection attacks. This paper presents a systematic evaluation of LLMs as academic reviewers. Using a curated dataset of 1,441 papers from ICLR 2023 and NeurIPS 2022, we evaluate GPT-5-mini against human reviewers across ratings, strengths, and weaknesses. The evaluation employs structured prompting with reference paper calibration, topic modeling, and similarity analysis to compare review content. We further embed covert instructions into PDF submissions to assess LLMs' susceptibility to prompt injection. Our findings show that LLMs consistently inflate ratings for weaker papers while aligning more closely with human judgments on stronger contributions. Moreover, while overarching malicious prompts induce only minor shifts in topical focus, explicitly field-specific instructions successfully manipulate specific aspects of LLM-generated reviews. This study underscores both the promises and perils of integrating LLMs into peer review and points to the importance of designing safeguards that ensure integrity and trust in future review processes.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "When Your Reviewer is an LLM: Biases, Divergence, and Prompt Injection Risks in Peer Review," focusing on novelty, significance, and a justified score:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as reviewers in academic peer review. It systematically evaluates LLMs (using GPT-3.5-mini) against human reviewers, analyzing ratings, identified strengths and weaknesses, and vulnerability to prompt injection attacks. The study uses a dataset of ICLR 2023 and NeurIPS 2022 papers and compares LLM-generated reviews with structured human reviews. The key findings are that LLMs tend to inflate ratings for lower-quality papers, exhibit differences in evaluative focus compared to human reviewers, and are susceptible to manipulation through embedded malicious prompts. The paper concludes by discussing the implications for policy and design to reconcile the utility and vulnerability of LLM-assisted review, advocating for a calibrated assistant approach rather than full replacement of human judgment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper is novel in its comprehensive and systematic approach to evaluating LLMs in peer review. While previous work has explored LLMs as reviewer aids, this study provides a more detailed analysis of biases, divergences from human reviewers, and, importantly, the vulnerability to prompt injection. The exploration of font manipulation for stealthy injection and field-specific injection to coerce extreme ratings and suppress weaknesses is a notable contribution. This adds new levels of risks and exploitation methods to the field of LLM prompt injection.

*   **Significance:** The study has significant implications for the integrity and future of academic peer review. As LLMs are increasingly used (knowingly or unknowingly) in the peer review process, understanding their limitations and vulnerabilities is crucial. The findings highlight potential biases that could lead to unfair acceptance decisions and demonstrate the serious threat of prompt injection attacks. The paper's recommendations for policy and design provide a valuable starting point for developing safeguards and guidelines for the responsible integration of LLMs in peer review. The research provides concrete evidence and analysis to inform policies and system designs to preserve peer review integrity while still leveraging potential efficiencies that LLMs can offer.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Systematic and Comprehensive Evaluation
    *   In-depth Analysis of LLM Biases and Divergences
    *   Investigation of Font Manipulation Prompt Injection Techniques
    *   Relevant Policy and Design Recommendations

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited to one LLM (GPT-3.5-mini, with some comparison to GPT-4.0-mini in the injection scenario) . Results may not generalize to all LLMs. Although it is acknowledged, testing on other LLMs could reinforce the validity of their claims.
    *   The data are limited to computer science venues (ICLR and NeurIPS). Results might vary across different disciplines with varying review cultures.

*   **Justification:** The paper provides a strong empirical foundation for its claims, using a large dataset and rigorous analysis. The identification of biases, divergence, and prompt injection risks is well-supported by the data. The discussion of policy and design implications is thoughtful and practical, providing concrete recommendations for addressing the challenges identified.
*   **Impact:** This is the most important part. This research gives a first look at the potential dangers LLMs can pose to Peer Reviews. The fact that even weaker LLMs are vulnerable to such basic attacks will likely affect all future LLM peer review strategies, highlighting just how important prompt injections are and will be in the future.

**Score: 8**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the understanding of the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in peer review. The exploration of font manipulation for stealthy injection and field-specific injection for extreme ratings is groundbreaking for LLM exploit research. This demonstrates the serious threat of prompt injection attacks and offers valuable insights for mitigating potential risks and preserving integrity, with the impact going further than a single experiment and opening a new branch of study. The primary reason for not assigning a higher score is the limitation of the study to a single LLM and the relatively narrow focus on computer science venues.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 8/10

### WALL: A Web Application for Automated Quality Assurance using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09918v1)
- **Authors**: Seyed Moein Abtahi, Akramul Azim
- **Abstract**: As software projects become increasingly complex, the volume and variety of issues in code files have grown substantially. Addressing this challenge requires efficient issue detection, resolution, and evaluation tools. This paper presents WALL, a web application that integrates SonarQube and large language models (LLMs) such as GPT-3.5 Turbo and GPT-4o to automate these tasks. WALL comprises three modules: an issue extraction tool, code issues reviser, and code comparison tool. Together, they enable a seamless pipeline for detecting software issues, generating automated code revisions, and evaluating the accuracy of revisions. Our experiments, conducted on 563 files with over 7,599 issues, demonstrate WALL's effectiveness in reducing human effort while maintaining high-quality revisions. Results show that employing a hybrid approach of cost-effective and advanced LLMs can significantly lower costs and improve revision rates. Future work aims to enhance WALL's capabilities by integrating open-source LLMs and eliminating human intervention, paving the way for fully automated code quality management.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents WALL, a web application designed to automate software quality assurance by integrating static code analysis (SonarQube) and large language models (LLMs). WALL consists of three modules: issue extraction, code revision using LLMs, and code comparison/evaluation. The paper demonstrates the application's ability to detect, revise, and evaluate code issues, reducing human effort while maintaining revision quality. The experiments on proprietary and open-source code bases show the effectiveness of using a hybrid approach, combining cost-effective LLMs (GPT-3.5 Turbo) with more advanced models (GPT-40) for complex issues. The authors also highlight the future direction of their work, which involves integrating open-source LLMs, eliminating human intervention for a fully automated pipeline, and using Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Practical Application:** The paper presents a practical application of LLMs to a real-world problem: automating code quality assurance. The integration of SonarQube for static analysis with LLMs for code revision is a logical and potentially effective approach.
    * **Modular Design:** The division of the application into three modules (issue extraction, code revision, and code comparison) provides a clear structure and allows for future extensions or modifications.
    * **Empirical Evaluation:** The authors conducted experiments on a substantial number of files and issues, providing empirical evidence for the effectiveness of their approach. The comparison between GPT-3.5 Turbo and GPT-40 provides valuable insights into the trade-offs between cost and performance.
    * **Hybrid Approach:** The proposed hybrid approach (using GPT-3.5 Turbo for most revisions and GPT-40 for complex ones) is a sensible strategy to optimize both cost and performance.
    * **Clear Future Directions:** The paper clearly outlines future directions for research, including the integration of open-source LLMs, the use of RAG, automation of the evaluation process, and fine-tuning of LLMs.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty in Core Idea:** Using LLMs for code generation/revision is not a completely new idea. Many existing works explore similar concepts. The novelty here lies primarily in the specific implementation and the integration with SonarQube in a pipeline.
    * **Dependence on Proprietary Data:** The main experiment relies on proprietary code from Team Eagle Ltd., which limits the reproducibility and generalizability of the results. While understandable, it is a significant limitation. The open-source test is less comprehensive and less emphasized.
    * **Evaluation Metric Limitations:**  The evaluation metrics focus on lines changed (precision, recall, F1-score).  This is a very simple metric, which doesn't necessarily correlate with the correctness or quality of the revision.  A more robust evaluation would require unit tests or functional testing of the revised code. The paper acknowledges the absence of test cases but this significantly weakens the evaluation's reliability.
    * **Lack of Error Analysis:** While the paper reports overall success rates, it lacks a detailed analysis of the types of errors made by the LLMs. Understanding these errors is crucial for improving the system's accuracy and reliability.
    * **Limited Detail on Prompt Engineering:** The paper mentions a structured prompt engineering process, but it lacks specific details about the prompt used.  Sharing the prompt would significantly enhance the paper's reproducibility and allow other researchers to build upon their work.
    * **Cost Calculation Transparency:** While cost figures are given, the exact methodology for computing these costs from OpenAI API usage isn't rigorously detailed, making it hard to verify.
* **Significance:**
    * The paper addresses a relevant and important problem: automating software quality assurance.
    * It provides a practical implementation of LLMs for code revision, demonstrating the potential for reducing human effort.
    * The evaluation provides valuable insights into the trade-offs between different LLMs and the benefits of a hybrid approach.
    * The paper outlines a clear roadmap for future research, which could lead to more effective and automated code quality assurance tools.

**Overall Assessment:**

While the core idea isn't entirely novel, the paper presents a well-engineered application with a solid empirical evaluation. The use of a hybrid LLM approach and the integration with SonarQube are valuable contributions. However, the reliance on proprietary data, limitations in evaluation metrics, lack of error analysis, and missing prompt details weaken the paper's overall impact. The work is a solid engineering contribution that showcases how LLMs can be deployed in software engineering workflows.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

A score of 6.5 reflects the paper's practical contribution and empirical evaluation, balanced against the limited novelty, dependence on proprietary data, and weaknesses in the evaluation methodology. The paper provides a good starting point for future research in this area, but further work is needed to address the limitations and improve the system's accuracy and reliability. It demonstrates a promising application of LLMs, but lacks some of the deeper theoretical or methodological contributions that would warrant a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Fraud detection and risk assessment of online payment transactions on e-commerce platforms based on LLM and GCN frameworks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09928v1)
- **Authors**: RuiHan Luo, Nanxi Wang, Xiaotong Zhu
- **Abstract**: With the rapid growth of e-commerce, online payment fraud has become increasingly complex, posing serious threats to financial security and consumer trust. Traditional detection methods often struggle to capture the intricate relational structures inherent in transactional data. This study presents a novel fraud detection framework that combines Large Language Models (LLM) with Graph Convolutional Networks (GCN) to effectively identify fraudulent activities in e-commerce online payment transactions. A dataset of 2,840,000 transactions was collected over 14 days from major platforms such as Amazon, involving approximately 2,000 U.S.-based consumers and 30 merchants. With fewer than 6000 fraudulent instances, the dataset represents a highly imbalanced scenario. Consumers and merchants were modeled as nodes and transactions as edges to form a heterogeneous graph, upon which a GCN was applied to learn complex behavioral patterns. Semantic features extracted via GPT-4o and Tabformer were integrated with structural features to enhance detection performance. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model achieves an accuracy of 0.98, effectively balancing precision and sensitivity in fraud detection. This framework offers a scalable and real-time solution for securing online payment environments and provides a promising direction for applying graph-based deep learning in financial fraud prevention.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel fraud detection framework for e-commerce online payments. It integrates Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-4o, with Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) to identify fraudulent transactions. The approach models user-merchant interactions as a heterogeneous graph, enriched with both structured and unstructured data (merchant category codes, transaction amounts, user behavior, etc.), leveraging LLMs to extract semantic features. Experiments on a large dataset of real-world transactions from platforms like Amazon demonstrate that the model achieves high accuracy and precision, although recall is lower, and significantly minimizing false negatives in identifying fraud.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The combination of LLMs and GCNs for fraud detection in e-commerce is reasonably novel. While both GCNs and LLMs have been individually applied to fraud detection, the specific *integration* for this *particular problem* appears less explored. The use of GPT-4o specifically, to extract semantic information from unstructured data related to e-commerce transactions provides some novelty, although LLMs have previously been leveraged to enrich fraud analysis in related contexts.  The core novelty hinges on the specific architecture and feature engineering choices in *how* the LLM and GCN are combined and their specific application to the e-commerce transaction domain.  A stronger paper would have explicitly compared this novel integration to other existing combinations of graph-based and deep-learning fraud detection methods.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant real-world problem: the increasing sophistication of online payment fraud. The potential benefits of a more accurate and scalable fraud detection system are substantial, affecting both consumers and businesses. Minimizing false negatives is a critical goal. Given e-commerce fraud is always evolving, contributions that address this challenge are relevant. The result of minimizing false negatives is a strength, yet the trade-off with lowering recall might still raise concerns regarding the impact on legitimate customers flagged as suspicious.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper combines multiple techniques (LLMs for semantic understanding, GCNs for graph-based analysis) to address the problem.
    * **Real-world Data:** The experiments are conducted on a relatively large, real-world dataset.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the proposed method and results.
    * **Addresses Imbalance:** The paper acknowledges and addresses the class imbalance problem inherent in fraud detection.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Comparison:** The paper lacks a robust comparison to state-of-the-art fraud detection methods. While it mentions related work, it doesn't provide a thorough empirical comparison to existing LLM based, GCN based and other traditional approaches, making it hard to truly assess the relative advantages of the proposed method.
    * **Recall Limitations:** The lower recall score is a concern, indicating that the model is missing a significant portion of fraudulent transactions, even if false negatives are low.  A more nuanced discussion around the implications of this trade-off is necessary. The paper should include more details on the kinds of fraud being missed in the recall portion and the business or customer cost of missing fraud and flagging legitimate transactions.
    * **GPT-4o dependence:** The strong reliance on GPT-4o is a potential limitation. The availability and cost of using such a resource could impact the practicality of the approach in different environments. The model is highly dependent on the availability and accessibility of proprietary LLMs.
    * **Limited Ablation studies:** The paper lacks ablation studies that validate the specific contributions of different components, making it difficult to understand the relative importance of the LLM and GCN components. For instance, what is the impact of feature representation without using LLM, is the GCN architecture superior to other graph-based learning, etc.
    * **Generalizability:** Although the dataset includes transactions from major platforms, the model's generalizability to different e-commerce ecosystems should be verified.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper's influence could be significant if the proposed method is shown to consistently outperform existing approaches in diverse settings and the practical challenges relating to GPT-40 accessibility can be resolved.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and potentially significant approach to fraud detection. However, several weaknesses, particularly the limited comparative evaluation and relatively lower recall, hold back its potential. The lack of a more rigorous ablation and a stronger comparison to the prior art significantly impacts the assessment of the specific contribution of the paper. Therefore, a score of 6 is appropriate. It's a good starting point with promising aspects, but needs substantial improvements.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CE
- **Score**: 6/10

### SmartCoder-R1: Towards Secure and Explainable Smart Contract Generation with Security-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09942v1)
- **Authors**: Lei Yu, Jingyuan Zhang, Xin Wang, Jiajia Ma, Li Yang, Fengjun Zhang
- **Abstract**: Smart contracts automate the management of high-value assets, where vulnerabilities can lead to catastrophic financial losses. This challenge is amplified in Large Language Models (LLMs) by two interconnected failures: they operate as unauditable "black boxes" lacking a transparent reasoning process, and consequently, generate code riddled with critical security vulnerabilities. To address both issues, we propose SmartCoder-R1 (based on Qwen2.5-Coder-7B), a novel framework for secure and explainable smart contract generation. It begins with Continual Pre-training (CPT) to specialize the model. We then apply Long Chain-of-Thought Supervised Fine-Tuning (L-CoT SFT) on 7,998 expert-validated reasoning-and-code samples to train the model to emulate human security analysis. Finally, to directly mitigate vulnerabilities, we employ Security-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (S-GRPO), a reinforcement learning phase that refines the generation policy by optimizing a weighted reward signal for compilation success, security compliance, and format correctness. Evaluated against 17 baselines on a benchmark of 756 real-world functions, SmartCoder-R1 establishes a new state of the art, achieving top performance across five key metrics: a ComPass of 87.70%, a VulRate of 8.60%, a SafeAval of 80.16%, a FuncRate of 53.84%, and a FullRate of 50.53%. This FullRate marks a 45.79% relative improvement over the strongest baseline, DeepSeek-R1. Crucially, its generated reasoning also excels in human evaluations, achieving high-quality ratings for Functionality (82.7%), Security (85.3%), and Clarity (90.7%).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SmartCoder-R1, a novel framework for generating secure and explainable smart contracts based on the Qwen2.5-Coder-7B model. The framework uses a three-stage pipeline consisting of: 1) Continual Pre-training (CPT) on Solidity code, 2) Long Chain-of-Thought Supervised Fine-Tuning (L-CoT SFT) to teach the model to emulate human security analysis and explain its reasoning, and 3) Security-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (S-GRPO) to directly mitigate vulnerabilities through reinforcement learning. The S-GRPO phase optimizes for compilation success, security compliance, and format correctness. Evaluated on a benchmark of real-world functions, SmartCoder-R1 achieves state-of-the-art results across key metrics like ComPass (compilability), VulRate (vulnerability rate), SafeAval (compilable and secure rate), FuncRate (functional correctness), and FullRate (all criteria met). The generated reasoning is also shown to be high-quality through human evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a well-integrated pipeline, combining pre-training, supervised fine-tuning with chain-of-thought, and reinforcement learning in a security-aware manner. The S-GRPO component appears to be a significant contribution, as it directly targets vulnerability mitigation during code generation, unlike post-hoc auditing approaches. The concept of using a group relative policy optimization tailored for security is quite innovative. The authors also contribute with their expert-validated datasets which are designed to promote and facilitate open research in explainable smart contract generation.

*   **Significance:** The smart contract domain is highly sensitive to security vulnerabilities, and the potential financial losses are significant. Automated code generation for smart contracts is a promising avenue, but the generated code *must* be secure. The paper's focus on explainability is also important, as it promotes trust and auditability, addressing the "black box" issue common in LLMs. The significant performance gains over existing models, particularly in reducing vulnerability rates and improving the "FullRate" metric, demonstrate the practical value of the approach. The released datasets could become valuable resources for the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The three-stage pipeline is thoughtfully designed, addressing different aspects of the problem (domain knowledge, reasoning, and security).
    *   The S-GRPO component is a particularly strong contribution.
    *   The extensive experimental evaluation demonstrates a clear performance advantage over a wide range of baselines.
    *   Human evaluation confirms the quality of the generated reasoning.
    *   The released datasets are a significant contribution to the research community, fostering reproducibility and future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper reports improved fullrate by 45.79%, a significant percentage increase, the model only achieved a 50.53% full rate. While this is better than existing baselines, there is still considerable room for future research that improves upon both safety and performance in complex smart contracts.
    *   The paper mentions a conservative approach to vulnerability detection but does not deeply examine false positive rates. Further work detailing the composition and curation of these checks and how to refine those signals is valuable to the research community.
    *   While the authors provide an ablation study demonstrating that all the key metrics are essential, a more detailed analysis of the failure cases (types of vulnerabilities that still persist, reasons for functional incorrectness) would provide further insights into potential areas for improvement.
    *   The experimental results are all reported on the same dataset which may introduce some bias. Testing generalization across different datasets would enhance credibility.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in automated smart contract generation. The focus on security and explainability aligns well with the needs of the domain, and the strong experimental results will encourage further investigation into RL-based vulnerability mitigation strategies. The released datasets could stimulate a wave of research in explainable and secure code generation.

*   **Justification for Score:** The SmartCoder-R1 architecture is well-designed and shows remarkable performance metrics with respect to existing baselines. However, the model still only achieves a FullRate of 50.53% and is still sensitive to more complex logic vulnerabilities. Although promising, it is likely there will be future research that improves on this model in the future, so the paper doesn't represent a "perfect" final solution.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Toward Green Code: Prompting Small Language Models for Energy-Efficient Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09947v1)
- **Authors**: Humza Ashraf, Syed Muhammad Danish, Zeeshan Sattar
- **Abstract**: There is a growing concern about the environmental impact of large language models (LLMs) in software development, particularly due to their high energy use and carbon footprint. Small Language Models (SLMs) offer a more sustainable alternative, requiring fewer computational resources while remaining effective for fundamental programming tasks. In this study, we investigate whether prompt engineering can improve the energy efficiency of SLMs in code generation. We evaluate four open-source SLMs, StableCode-Instruct-3B, Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Instruct, CodeLlama-7B-Instruct, and Phi-3-Mini-4K-Instruct, across 150 Python problems from LeetCode, evenly distributed into easy, medium, and hard categories. Each model is tested under four prompting strategies: role prompting, zero-shot, few-shot, and chain-of-thought (CoT). For every generated solution, we measure runtime, memory usage, and energy consumption, comparing the results with a human-written baseline. Our findings show that CoT prompting provides consistent energy savings for Qwen2.5-Coder and StableCode-3B, while CodeLlama-7B and Phi-3-Mini-4K fail to outperform the baseline under any prompting strategy. These results highlight that the benefits of prompting are model-dependent and that carefully designed prompts can guide SLMs toward greener software development.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Toward Green Code: Prompting Small Language Models for Energy-Efficient Code Generation" investigates whether prompt engineering can improve the energy efficiency of small language models (SLMs) when generating code. The authors evaluate four open-source SLMs (StableCode-Instruct-3B, Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Instruct, CodeLlama-7B-Instruct, and Phi-3-Mini-4K-Instruct) across 150 Python problems from LeetCode, using four different prompting strategies: role prompting, zero-shot, few-shot, and chain-of-thought (CoT). They measure runtime, memory usage, and energy consumption for each generated solution, comparing the results to a human-written baseline. The key finding is that CoT prompting provides consistent energy savings for Qwen2.5-Coder and StableCode-3B, while CodeLlama-7B and Phi-3-Mini-4K do not consistently outperform the baseline with any prompting strategy. The authors conclude that the benefits of prompting are model-dependent, and carefully designed prompts are crucial for guiding SLMs toward greener software development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies in its focused investigation on prompt engineering for *small* language models specifically for energy-efficient code generation. While previous studies have explored prompt engineering for LLMs to improve code quality and sustainability, fewer works examine this in the context of resource-constrained SLMs. The specific comparison of the four SLMs with a variety of prompting techniques to explore efficiency is a reasonably novel aspect.

*Significance:*

The paper addresses an increasingly important issue: the environmental impact of AI in software development. By exploring ways to make code generation more energy-efficient using SLMs and prompt engineering, the authors contribute to the development of more sustainable AI practices. Identifying that some prompt strategies work better with certain models, and specifically finding benefits in smaller models offers potentially useful information for developers.

*Strengths:*

*   **Clear Research Question and Methodology:** The paper clearly articulates its research question and employs a well-defined methodology for evaluating the performance and energy efficiency of SLMs.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study considers multiple SLMs, prompting strategies, and problem difficulties, providing a comprehensive analysis of the factors influencing energy efficiency.
*   **Focus on SLMs:** The focus on SLMs is a strong point, as it addresses a gap in the existing literature. SLMs are valuable in resource-constrained environments.
*   **Reproducible Experiments:** The authors clearly state the environment and setup which facilitates further research.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses solely on Python and LeetCode problems. This limits the generalizability of the findings to other programming languages and more complex software development scenarios.
*   **Lack of Deeper Analysis:** While the paper presents quantitative results for runtime, memory usage, and energy consumption, it lacks a deeper qualitative analysis of the generated code. For example, the paper does not discuss the quality of the generated code, or the type of optimizations performed by the SLMs in the generated code.  A study of the code optimization techniques and the reasons behind energy consumption variations would significantly strengthen the work.
*   **Baseline Quality:** While the paper uses community upvoted solutions, the impact of the specific selection of those solutions is not addressed. If the solution selected are inefficient from an energy perspective, this could reduce the impact of the paper.
*   **GPT-5 dependence:** The dependency on GPT-5 for CoT prompting introduces an external dependency and potential variable in the process. While the strategy itself might be good, reliance on an external tool means that results could shift based on changes to GPT-5. This has implications for reproducibility.
*   **Lack of statistical significance:** The paper provides averages, but omits statistical significance tests. Statistical tests would help demonstrate that the differences observed are unlikely due to chance.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence research and practice in several ways:

*   **Guiding SLM Selection:** The study provides practical guidance on which SLMs and prompting strategies are best suited for energy-efficient code generation.
*   **Promoting Sustainable AI:** The paper raises awareness about the environmental impact of AI and encourages the development of more sustainable software development practices.
*   **Inspiring Further Research:** The findings can serve as a starting point for future research on prompt engineering, SLMs, and energy-efficient code generation.

*Justification for Score:*

While the paper tackles an important issue and provides useful insights into the energy efficiency of SLMs in code generation, it has several limitations that reduce its overall impact. The limited scope (Python and LeetCode), lack of in-depth qualitative analysis, and omission of statistical significance tests weaken the generalizability and rigor of the findings. While providing a good starting point, the dependence on GPT-5 for CoT prompting and the lack of deeper analysis of code optimization also lower the impact.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Byte by Byte: Unmasking Browser Fingerprinting at the Function Level Using V8 Bytecode Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09950v1)
- **Authors**: Pouneh Nikkhah Bahrami, Dylan Cutler, Igor Bilogrevic
- **Abstract**: Browser fingerprinting enables persistent cross-site user tracking via subtle techniques that often evade conventional defenses or cause website breakage when script-level blocking countermeasures are applied. Addressing these challenges requires detection methods offering both function-level precision to minimize breakage and inherent robustness against code obfuscation and URL manipulation. We introduce ByteDefender, the first system leveraging V8 engine bytecode to detect fingerprinting operations specifically at the JavaScript function level. A Transformer-based classifier, trained offline on bytecode sequences, accurately identifies functions exhibiting fingerprinting behavior. We develop and evaluate light-weight signatures derived from this model to enable low-overhead, on-device matching against function bytecode during compilation but prior to execution, which only adds a 4% (average) latency to the page load time. This mechanism facilitates targeted, real-time prevention of fingerprinting function execution, thereby preserving legitimate script functionality. Operating directly on bytecode ensures inherent resilience against common code obfuscation and URL-based evasion. Our evaluation on the top 100k websites demonstrates high detection accuracy at both function- and script-level, with substantial improvements over state-of-the-art AST-based methods, particularly in robustness against obfuscation. ByteDefender offers a practical framework for effective, precise, and robust fingerprinting mitigation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Byte by Byte: Unmasking Browser Fingerprinting at the Function Level Using V8 Bytecode Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ByteDefender, a novel system for detecting and mitigating browser fingerprinting.  Unlike existing approaches, ByteDefender operates at the JavaScript function level by analyzing V8 engine bytecode *before* execution. It uses a Transformer-based classifier, trained offline on bytecode sequences labeled using execution traces and heuristic rules, to identify functions exhibiting fingerprinting behavior.  This enables targeted prevention of fingerprinting, minimizing website breakage.  The system demonstrates resilience against code obfuscation and URL manipulation. The evaluation, conducted on a large dataset of websites, demonstrates improved accuracy compared to AST-based methods, with a low overhead on page load time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the **combination of several existing ideas in a specific and effective manner**. While individual components like bytecode analysis, Transformer models, and fingerprinting detection are not entirely new, their integration at the function level, pre-execution, with a focus on bytecode, and coupled with execution tracing for ground truth labeling, is a significant contribution. The function-level analysis is a key differentiator as it enables finer-grained intervention, reducing the web breakage. The use of bytecode directly addresses a critical weakness of AST-based methods: susceptibility to obfuscation.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a practical and important problem: mitigating browser fingerprinting without sacrificing website functionality.  The paper provides a robust solution capable of overcoming common evasion techniques that plague existing methods (obfuscation, URL manipulation). The significant improvement in detection accuracy and obfuscation resilience compared to prior AST-based approaches showcases the potential real-world impact of the work.  The low performance overhead (4% latency) makes the system practically deployable in browsers. While relying on heuristics for generating the ground truth may be a limitation, the overall performance gains outweigh this concern.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Function-level granularity reduces web breakage.
    *   Bytecode analysis provides inherent robustness against obfuscation.
    *   Pre-execution detection enables proactive mitigation.
    *   Demonstrated improvements over state-of-the-art AST-based techniques.
    *   Low performance overhead.
    *   Large-scale evaluation (100k websites).
    *   Publicly released implementation and codebase.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Ground truth generation relies on heuristics based on established fingerprinting techniques, potentially missing novel techniques.
    *   Exclusion of anonymous/`eval`-loaded functions during training *could* limit generalization, but the model's core bytecode analysis mitigates this to some degree during deployment. The paper states this is a focus of future research.
    *   V8-specific bytecode limits cross-browser applicability. The paper acknowledgeds this, and suggests avenues for addressing it.
    *   The long tail of possible evasions techniques may eventually lead to attacks against the model.

*   **Impact:** The paper provides a strong foundation for future research in bytecode-based browser fingerprinting detection. Its function-level analysis and proactive mitigation strategy are valuable contributions that can inform the design of more effective and privacy-preserving web browsers. The release of the code and dataset will facilitate further research and development in this area.

*   **Justification of Score:** Given the notable novelty in integrating existing ideas, the significant improvements over prior work in a critical area (obfuscation resilience), the practical implications for browser privacy, and the limitations discussed above, the paper warrants a relatively high score. The function-level granularity, bytecode analysis, and low overhead make it a significant step forward. However, the reliance on heuristics for ground truth labeling and the V8 engine dependency prevent a higher score.
