### Benchmark of stylistic variation in LLM-generated texts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10179v1)
- **Authors**: Jiří Milička, Anna Marklová, Václav Cvrček
- **Abstract**: This study investigates the register variation in texts written by humans and comparable texts produced by large language models (LLMs). Biber's multidimensional analysis (MDA) is applied to a sample of human-written texts and AI-created texts generated to be their counterparts to find the dimensions of variation in which LLMs differ most significantly and most systematically from humans. As textual material, a new LLM-generated corpus AI-Brown is used, which is comparable to BE-21 (a Brown family corpus representing contemporary British English). Since all languages except English are underrepresented in the training data of frontier LLMs, similar analysis is replicated on Czech using AI-Koditex corpus and Czech multidimensional model. Examined were 16 frontier models in various settings and prompts, with emphasis placed on the difference between base models and instruction-tuned models. Based on this, a benchmark is created through which models can be compared with each other and ranked in interpretable dimensions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a benchmark for evaluating stylistic variation in texts generated by Large Language Models (LLMs).  It applies Biber's Multi-Dimensional Analysis (MDA), a well-established technique in corpus linguistics, to compare the stylistic dimensions of LLM-generated texts with those of human-written texts.  The analysis is conducted on both English and Czech corpora, using a new LLM-generated corpus called AI-Brown (English) and AI-Koditex (Czech). The authors examined 16 frontier models in various settings and prompts, with a focus on differences between base models and instruction-tuned models. The goal is to create a replicable benchmark allowing for interpretable comparisons of LLM stylistic performance across different models and languages. The paper explores how well LLMs produce stylistically diverse texts, whether there's a consistent "AI style," how instruction tuning affects style, the impact of system prompts, and whether stylistic shifts differ across languages. The study presents a detailed methodology for corpus generation, MDA application, and statistical processing and the overall score of each model is expressed through the vector length, indicating how far a model's stylistic profile diverges from human text

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its *application* of a well-established linguistic method (MDA) to a new and relevant problem: assessing and quantifying the stylistic properties of LLM-generated text. While MDA is not a new technique, its systematic application to LLM outputs for benchmarking purposes, across languages, and with a focus on interpretability, is a valuable contribution. The development of new AI-generated corpora, AI-Brown and AI-Koditex, also adds a practical element. However, the underlying idea of comparing LLM outputs to human texts isn't inherently novel.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work stems from the growing importance of LLMs and the need to understand their capabilities and limitations. The paper addresses a crucial aspect of LLM performance: stylistic control and variation. By providing an interpretable benchmark, the authors offer a tool for LLM developers to evaluate and compare models in terms of their ability to mimic or generate diverse writing styles.  The multi-lingual aspect is also significant, highlighting the challenges of non-English language generation. The study shows how stylistic performance degrades in languages underrepresented in LLM training data. This is particularly important in the context of the growing importance of languages that are not commonly trained within an AI context.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper details a clear and reproducible methodology, allowing other researchers to replicate and extend the work.
    *   **Interpretable Results:** The use of MDA provides results that are readily interpretable in terms of established stylistic dimensions.
    *   **Multi-Lingual Focus:** The comparison between English and Czech provides valuable insights into the challenges of stylistic control across languages.
    *   **Emphasis on Benchmarking:** The explicit goal of creating a benchmark is a practical contribution to the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Exploration:** The number of models analyzed, while significant, could be expanded in future work to provide a more comprehensive benchmark.
    *   **Potential Data Contamination:** Although the authors mention they cannot rule out that the original corpora were included in the training data of models, this poses a limitation to the analysis as it may provide more stylistic similarity by accident.
    *   **Focus on Existing Dimensions:**  MDA, while useful, relies on predefined stylistic dimensions. The AI literature on emergent language suggests that LLMs might exhibit stylistic features not captured by traditional MDA.
    *   **Vector Length Metric:** Reducing the stylistic comparison to a single vector length metric, while providing a simple overall score, potentially oversimplifies the nuanced stylistic variations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development and evaluation of LLMs, particularly in the area of stylistic control and cross-lingual generation.  It can serve as a starting point for future research on developing more nuanced and expressive LLMs. Also, future applications can be developed by expanding the method, particularly by looking into how specific stylistic preferences can be produced consistently by prompt manipulation.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by systematically applying a linguistic method to the growing field of LLM research. The work is well-executed, clearly presented, and offers practical insights for LLM developers. However, the limitations regarding the scope of model exploration and the reliance on pre-defined stylistic dimensions temper its overall impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Incongruent Positivity: When Miscalibrated Positivity Undermines Online Supportive Conversations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10184v1)
- **Authors**: Leen Almajed, Abeer ALdayel
- **Abstract**: In emotionally supportive conversations, well-intended positivity can sometimes misfire, leading to responses that feel dismissive, minimizing, or unrealistically optimistic. We examine this phenomenon of incongruent positivity as miscalibrated expressions of positive support in both human and LLM generated responses. To this end, we collected real user-assistant dialogues from Reddit across a range of emotional intensities and generated additional responses using large language models for the same context. We categorize these conversations by intensity into two levels: Mild, which covers relationship tension and general advice, and Severe, which covers grief and anxiety conversations. This level of categorization enables a comparative analysis of how supportive responses vary across lower and higher stakes contexts. Our analysis reveals that LLMs are more prone to unrealistic positivity through dismissive and minimizing tone, particularly in high-stakes contexts. To further study the underlying dimensions of this phenomenon, we finetune LLMs on datasets with strong and weak emotional reactions. Moreover, we developed a weakly supervised multilabel classifier ensemble (DeBERTa and MentalBERT) that shows improved detection of incongruent positivity types across two sorts of concerns (Mild and Severe). Our findings shed light on the need to move beyond merely generating generic positive responses and instead study the congruent support measures to balance positive affect with emotional acknowledgment. This approach offers insights into aligning large language models with affective expectations in the online supportive dialogue, paving the way toward context-aware and trust preserving online conversation systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper tackles the phenomenon of "incongruent positivity" in supportive conversations, where well-intentioned positive responses from both humans and Large Language Models (LLMs) misfire by appearing dismissive, minimizing, or unrealistically optimistic. The study investigates this issue by analyzing real and LLM-generated dialogues from Reddit across two concern levels (Mild and Severe). It identifies that LLMs are more prone to generating unrealistically positive responses, especially in high-stakes contexts. The authors fine-tune LLMs on datasets with varying emotional reactions and develop a weakly supervised multi-label classifier to detect different types of incongruent positivity. The findings emphasize the need to move beyond simply generating positive responses and focus on context-aware and emotionally aligned support in online conversation systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of "incongruent positivity" is a valuable contribution, framing a common problem in human-computer interaction and NLP. The study extends beyond simple sentiment analysis by categorizing different types of misguided positivity (Dismissive, Minimizing, Unrealistic) and considering the emotional intensity of the context. The exploration of "Relatability" as a reframing strategy is also new and valuable.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have implications for the design of more effective and trustworthy online support systems. Understanding and mitigating incongruent positivity is crucial as LLMs are increasingly used in emotionally sensitive contexts. The dataset created and the weakly supervised classifier developed are valuable resources for future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   The paper addresses a practically relevant problem.
*   The methodology combines real-world data analysis with LLM experimentation.
*   The annotation scheme captures nuanced aspects of emotional support.
*   The weakly supervised classifier offers a promising approach for detecting incongruent positivity.
*   The inclusion of human responder data provides a crucial comparison point.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The reliance on Reddit data may limit the generalizability of the findings to other online platforms.
*   The inter-annotator agreement in the Severe Concern context is relatively low, indicating potential challenges in consistently identifying incongruent positivity in high-stakes situations.
*   The success of fine-tuning could be explored in more depth and compared to other alignment techniques.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While the concept of incongruent positivity is novel and the work provides a valuable dataset and initial experiments, the findings are somewhat preliminary. The experiments show fine-tuning on emotional reaction data reduces some miscalibrated positivity and highlights the value of this type of data but the classification accuracy still leaves much to be desired.

### P3D: Scalable Neural Surrogates for High-Resolution 3D Physics Simulations with Global Context
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10186v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Holzschuh, Georg Kohl, Florian Redinger, Nils Thuerey
- **Abstract**: We present a scalable framework for learning deterministic and probabilistic neural surrogates for high-resolution 3D physics simulations. We introduce a hybrid CNN-Transformer backbone architecture targeted for 3D physics simulations, which significantly outperforms existing architectures in terms of speed and accuracy. Our proposed network can be pretrained on small patches of the simulation domain, which can be fused to obtain a global solution, optionally guided via a fast and scalable sequence-to-sequence model to include long-range dependencies. This setup allows for training large-scale models with reduced memory and compute requirements for high-resolution datasets. We evaluate our backbone architecture against a large set of baseline methods with the objective to simultaneously learn the dynamics of 14 different types of PDEs in 3D. We demonstrate how to scale our model to high-resolution isotropic turbulence with spatial resolutions of up to $512^3$. Finally, we demonstrate the versatility of our network by training it as a diffusion model to produce probabilistic samples of highly turbulent 3D channel flows across varying Reynolds numbers, accurately capturing the underlying flow statistics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "P3D: Scalable Neural Surrogates for High-Resolution 3D Physics Simulations with Global Context":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces P3D, a novel and scalable framework for learning neural surrogates for high-resolution 3D physics simulations, particularly fluid dynamics. The key components are:

1.  **A hybrid CNN-Transformer architecture (P3D):** Combines CNNs for local feature extraction with windowed self-attention via transformers for deep representation learning.
2.  **Scalability:**  P3D can be pretrained on smaller patches of the simulation domain and then scaled up to the full domain, reducing memory and computational costs.
3.  **Global Context Integration:** Utilizes a sequence-to-sequence model (context model) to link bottleneck layers, enabling efficient global information processing and handling long-range dependencies. The global information is injected into the decoder via region tokens and adaptive instance normalization.
4.  **Applications:** The authors demonstrate P3D's effectiveness across diverse experiments, including learning multiple PDE dynamics simultaneously, modeling isotropic turbulence at resolutions up to 512³, and generating probabilistic samples of turbulent channel flows.

The paper also explores different finetuning methodologies that enable a more fine-grained control of memory and compute.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Architecture:** The hybrid CNN-Transformer design is a valuable contribution. Combining the strengths of CNNs (local feature extraction) and Transformers (long-range dependencies) makes sense for physics simulations. The windowed attention mechanism is a good choice to mitigate the quadratic complexity of global self-attention in 3D. The integration of a sequence-to-sequence model for global context is another important novelty that addresses a critical need for surrogate models.
*   **Scalability Approach:**  The patch-based pretraining and scaling strategy addresses a crucial limitation of training neural networks on very large 3D datasets. This strategy allows the model to operate efficiently in high-resolution simulations.
*   **Global Context integration**: The specific mechanisms for context injection and processing are well-motivated and contribute to the paper's novelty.
*   **Finetuning methodologies:** The proposed setups for memory-efficient training during finetuning are valuable for practical applications where resources are limited.

**Significance:**

*   **High-Resolution 3D Simulations:**  The paper directly tackles a challenging and important problem: creating efficient surrogates for computationally expensive 3D physics simulations. Scaling to 512³ resolution represents a substantial advance.
*   **Versatility:** Demonstrating success across 14 PDEs and two distinct fluid dynamics problems highlights the generalizability of the P3D framework.
*   **Probabilistic Modeling:** Training P3D as a diffusion model for turbulent channel flows is significant. It allows for generating diverse, physically plausible samples and estimating uncertainty.
*   **Potential Impact:** The work has the potential to accelerate scientific discovery by enabling faster exploration of parameter spaces, uncertainty quantification, and real-time predictions in various fields, including fluid dynamics, aerospace, and climate science.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Experimental Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive set of experiments with detailed quantitative results.  The comparisons against multiple baselines clearly demonstrate the superiority of P3D.
*   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the architecture, training methodologies, and experimental setup.
*   **Code Availability (Implied):** The authors suggest that the source code of their method will be public in the camera-ready paper, which further enhances the paper's value.
*   **Addresses an important challenge:** The study specifically tackles a critical hurdle in adopting machine learning for scientific simulations – scaling to high-resolution 3D datasets.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While P3D offers improvements in memory and speed, the actual training and inference costs at very high resolutions remain substantial. It would be useful to provide a detailed breakdown of training time and hardware requirements for the largest simulations. The scalability analysis, while promising, is limited by a single, relatively small-scale, isotropic turbulence example.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the architectural choices are well-motivated, the paper lacks a rigorous theoretical analysis of why P3D works well. For instance, a deeper understanding of the impact of window size, context model capacity, or choice of positional encodings could strengthen the work.
*   **Generality:** The excellent results with the 14 PDEs could be interpreted as a weakness: the dataset is limited to simulations on regular meshes (requiring the use of Exponax). What about real-world data (e.g., measurements) or simulations on unstructured meshes?
*   **Comparison to LLM-inspired attention architectures:** P3D is benchmarked only against models that were published in the past. Given the rapid advancement of attention architectures that use ideas inspired by LLMs, a comparison to these LLM-inspired attention architectures (e.g., Mamba, RetNet) is also important.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of scientific machine learning by introducing P3D, a novel and scalable architecture for learning neural surrogates for 3D physics simulations. The comprehensive experimental results, versatility, and potential impact on various scientific domains are impressive. However, the reliance on direct numerical simulations on regular meshes, the limited theoretical analysis, and the expensive computational costs prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG

### Beyond Token Limits: Assessing Language Model Performance on Long Text Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10199v1)
- **Authors**: Miklós Sebők, Viktor Kovács, Martin Bánóczy, Daniel Møller Eriksen, Nathalie Neptune, Philippe Roussille
- **Abstract**: The most widely used large language models in the social sciences (such as BERT, and its derivatives, e.g. RoBERTa) have a limitation on the input text length that they can process to produce predictions. This is a particularly pressing issue for some classification tasks, where the aim is to handle long input texts. One such area deals with laws and draft laws (bills), which can have a length of multiple hundred pages and, therefore, are not particularly amenable for processing with models that can only handle e.g. 512 tokens. In this paper, we show results from experiments covering 5 languages with XLM-RoBERTa, Longformer, GPT-3.5, GPT-4 models for the multiclass classification task of the Comparative Agendas Project, which has a codebook of 21 policy topic labels from education to health care. Results show no particular advantage for the Longformer model, pre-trained specifically for the purposes of handling long inputs. The comparison between the GPT variants and the best-performing open model yielded an edge for the latter. An analysis of class-level factors points to the importance of support and substance overlaps between specific categories when it comes to performance on long text inputs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of various language models on long text classification, specifically focusing on the Comparative Agendas Project (CAP) classification task.  It compares encoder models like XLM-ROBERTa (base and large), Longformer, and generative models like GPT-3.5, GPT-4 and Llama 3.  The study analyzes the impact of fine-tuning data composition (short vs. long texts), maximum input sequence length, and model architecture/size.  The key findings suggest that fine-tuning XLM-ROBERTa with a mixture of short and long texts yields superior results, that increasing the sequence length for Longformer does not significantly improve performance in this task, and that a larger XLM-ROBERTa model outperforms smaller models and Longformer. Finally, it concludes that Generative LLMs tested in Zero-shot and One-shot setting falls short of the performance of the fine-tuned encoder based models.  The paper also explores class-level misclassifications, highlighting the importance of high support categories and substance overlaps between categories.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper tackles a relevant and important issue: the limitations of standard language models on long text classification tasks within the social sciences. While the idea of using Longformer for long text is not entirely new, the paper contributes by systematically comparing it with XLM-ROBERTa and evaluating generative approaches for the CAP classification task across multiple languages.  The detailed class-level analysis, though not entirely novel in NLP, adds value in understanding model behavior in this specific domain.  The comparison of open and closed LLMs is also relevant, given their increasing popularity and the need for cost-benefit analysis.
* **Significance:** The study provides practical insights for researchers using language models for classifying long documents, particularly those involved in policy analysis or related fields. The findings regarding the effectiveness of mixed training data and the limited gains from increasing Longformer sequence length are valuable for guiding model selection and training strategies. The benchmark performance of the models presented in the study provides a helpful reference point for future research. The identification of challenging class overlaps contributes to a deeper understanding of the task's inherent complexity.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper conducts a thorough evaluation of multiple models and hyperparameter settings. The experimental design is well-defined, and the results are clearly presented.
    *   **Multilingual Focus:**  The inclusion of multiple languages (including a lower-resource language like Hungarian) strengthens the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The study addresses a real-world problem faced by social science researchers dealing with long policy documents.
    *   **Class-Level Analysis:** The analysis of class-level misclassifications provides valuable insights into model behavior and the task's inherent challenges.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Fine-tuning for Generative Models:** The reliance on zero-shot/one-shot classification for generative models might not fully leverage their capabilities. Fine-tuning GPT or Llama on the CAP task, while computationally expensive, could reveal whether these models can bridge the performance gap. However, the paper explicitly states this is due to cost concerns which, while understandable, does limit the strength of this particular comparison.
    *   **Validation set:** The validation data was sampled from the test set which may have led to overestimation of model performance.
    *   **Lack of comparison against other efficient transformer variants:** The paper dismisses approaches with Sliding Windows, Hierarchical Attention and other more recent compression approaches based on outdated performance and/or lack of pre-trained multilngual variants.
    *   **Longformer pre-trained data:** In section 2.4 the paper states that "at the time of writing, only Longformer offers a multilingual pre-trained model checkpoint." but this can be misleading. As stated later in the paper, the pre-trained weight of the LongFormer used is simply XLM-Roberta weights, which can explain some of the results. This could have been discussed more directly in the document.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence researchers working on policy analysis, digital humanities, and other fields where long text classification is crucial.  The study's findings will guide model selection, training strategies, and error analysis.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by systematically comparing various language models for a relevant task in the social sciences. While the methodological approach is not groundbreaking, the multi-lingual evaluation, detailed class-level analysis, and practical implications are valuable. However, the limitations in the Generative Model evaluation, along with the dismissal of more recent compression algorithms for performance reasons and some lack of clarity with LongFormer, detract from the paper's overall impact. A score of 6 reflects the paper's meaningful but not exceptional contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### SI-FACT: Mitigating Knowledge Conflict via Self-Improving Faithfulness-Aware Contrastive Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10208v1)
- **Authors**: Shengqiang Fu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models often generate unfaithful responses in knowledge intensive tasks due to knowledge conflict,that is,a preference for relying on internal parametric knowledge rather than the provided context.To address this issue,we propose a novel self improving framework,Self Improving Faithfulness Aware Contrastive Tuning.The framework uses a self instruct mechanism that allows the base LLM to automatically generate high quality,structured contrastive learning data,including anchor samples,semantically equivalent positive samples,and negative samples simulating unfaithful scenarios.This approach significantly reduces the cost of manual annotation.Subsequently,contrastive learning is applied to train the model,enabling it to pull faithful responses closer and push unfaithful responses farther apart in the representation space.Experiments on knowledge conflict evaluation benchmarks ECARE KRE and COSE KRE show that the SI FACT model based on Llama3 8B Instruct improves the Contextual Recall Rate by 6.2% over the best baseline method,while significantly reducing dependence on internal memory.The results indicate that SI FACT provides strong effectiveness and high data efficiency in enhancing the contextual faithfulness of LLMs,offering a practical pathway toward building more proactive and trustworthy language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SI-FACT (Self-Improving Faithfulness-Aware Contrastive Tuning), a novel framework designed to mitigate knowledge conflict in Large Language Models (LLMs). Knowledge conflict arises when LLMs prioritize internal parametric knowledge over the provided context, leading to unfaithful responses. SI-FACT addresses this by employing a self-instruction mechanism, where the LLM autonomously generates high-quality contrastive learning data (anchor, positive, and negative samples). This data is then used in contrastive learning to train the model to favor responses aligned with the input context. Experiments on knowledge conflict evaluation benchmarks demonstrate that SI-FACT significantly improves contextual recall rate (CRR) and reduces reliance on internal memory compared to baseline methods. The paper emphasizes SI-FACT's data efficiency and its potential for building more trustworthy language models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its self-improving framework for contrastive learning. While contrastive learning itself isn't new, the approach of using the LLM to automatically generate the contrastive dataset tailored for faithfulness is a significant contribution. This contrasts with traditional methods that rely on manual annotation or heuristic data augmentation. The framework offers a new perspective of LLMs that are active learners capable of Self-Improvement. The use of prompts to generate anchor, positive, and negative samples is a good approach. The generated samples cover 3 critical types of unfaithfulness.

*   **Significance:** The issue of knowledge conflict and unfaithful generation is a major hurdle for deploying LLMs in high-stakes applications. By addressing this directly and demonstrably improving contextual faithfulness, SI-FACT makes a valuable contribution to the field. The data efficiency aspect is also significant; the framework achieves strong results with relatively small amounts of training data, making it more practical and scalable. It also shows the potential of "model self-improving" paradigm for optimizing key capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Novel framework with a compelling self-improving mechanism.
    *   Strong experimental results on established benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of SI-FACT.
    *   High data efficiency compared to traditional fine-tuning approaches.
    *   Detailed analysis of the representation space, providing insights into how SI-FACT shapes the model's behavior.
    *   Analysis demonstrating preservation of general reasoning capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on SQuAD for generating anchor triplets could limit the framework's generalizability to other domains or task types where similar datasets are not available. The generation from a standard QA dataset might be too simple.
    *   While the performance on benchmark datasets is impressive, further investigation is needed to assess SI-FACT's robustness in real-world scenarios with noisy or ambiguous contexts.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the limitations of the self-instruction mechanism, especially potential biases introduced by the LLM's own knowledge. While negative samples are generated, it can further explain how the negative sample is chosen. How does LLM handle the conflict when it itself is being asked for a negative/conflicting sample?
    *   There could be more explanation of hyperparameter tuning.

*   **Potential Influence:** SI-FACT has the potential to significantly influence research on mitigating knowledge conflict and improving the trustworthiness of LLMs. The self-improving paradigm could inspire new approaches to training LLMs for various capabilities, leveraging their internal knowledge for automated data generation. The data efficiency of SI-FACT could make it a valuable tool for practitioners with limited resources.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in LLM research with a novel and well-evaluated framework. The improvements demonstrated on benchmark datasets are significant, and the data efficiency of the approach makes it practical. While some limitations exist regarding the reliance on SQuAD-style data and the need for further robustness testing, the overall contribution is substantial. It is a significant step toward trustworthiness and proactive language models.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### RFSeek and Ye Shall Find
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10216v1)
- **Authors**: Noga H. Rotman, Tiago Ferreira, Hila Peleg, Mark Silberstein, Alexandra Silva
- **Abstract**: Requests for Comments (RFCs) are extensive specification documents for network protocols, but their prose-based format and their considerable length often impede precise operational understanding. We present RFSeek, an interactive tool that automatically extracts visual summaries of protocol logic from RFCs. RFSeek leverages large language models (LLMs) to generate provenance-linked, explorable diagrams, surfacing both official state machines and additional logic found only in the RFC text. Compared to existing RFC visualizations, RFSeek's visual summaries are more transparent and easier to audit against their textual source. We showcase the tool's potential through a series of use cases, including guided knowledge extraction and semantic diffing, applied to protocols such as TCP, QUIC, PPTP, and DCCP. In practice, RFSeek not only reconstructs the RFC diagrams included in some specifications, but, more interestingly, also uncovers important logic such as nodes or edges described in the text but missing from those diagrams. RFSeek further derives new visualization diagrams for complex RFCs, with QUIC as a representative case. Our approach, which we term \emph{Summary Visualization}, highlights a promising direction: combining LLMs with formal, user-customized visualizations to enhance protocol comprehension and support robust implementations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RFSeek and Ye Shall Find: A tool for summary visualization and analysis of RFCs."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RFSeek, an interactive tool that automatically extracts visual summaries of network protocol logic from RFCs (Requests for Comments).  RFSeek uses large language models (LLMs) to generate provenance-linked, explorable diagrams, showcasing both official state machines and additional logic found only in the RFC text.  It goes beyond simply reproducing existing diagrams by identifying logic described in the text but absent from the diagrams.  The tool highlights the originating text within the RFC for each element in the diagram, enabling easier auditing and deeper understanding. Case studies on protocols like TCP, QUIC, PPTP, and DCCP demonstrate RFSeek's capabilities, including its ability to uncover hidden logic and create new visualization diagrams. The authors call their approach "Summary Visualization" and argue that it significantly enhances protocol comprehension and supports more robust implementations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of LLMs and interactive visualization to enhance understanding of RFCs.  While previous work has used NLP/LLMs for protocol analysis (fuzzing, attack synthesis, code generation, and model extraction), RFSeek's focus on accurate, auditable, and human-interpretable protocol representation is a key differentiator. Extracting *hidden* logic not explicitly visualized in existing diagrams is valuable. The paper provides a clear point-by-point comparison to PROSPER [10], a closely related LLM-based FSM extraction tool, underscoring RFSeek's advantages in grounding data points to the RFC, integrating broader RFC context and more reliably recovering diagrammed elements (especially for RFCs with incomplete diagrams like QUIC).

*   **Significance:**  RFCs are the cornerstone of internet standards, but their complexity and length can be a significant barrier to implementation and understanding. RFSeek has the potential to improve protocol development and deployment by making these specifications more accessible and transparent. Improving protocol understanding also has implications for network security, as ambiguities or omissions in RFCs can lead to vulnerabilities. The provision of additional transitions and states that are present within the text of the RFC but missing within the FSM diagram is also a very significant feature.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of working with RFCs and the need for better visualization and analysis tools.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** The summary representation and extraction pipeline are well-explained and justified. The modularity of the pipeline (structural summarization, visualization extraction, semantic grounding) facilitates improvements and adaptation to new protocols.
    *   **Strong Evaluation:** The case studies showcase the tool's effectiveness and demonstrate its ability to uncover hidden protocol logic.  The comparison to PROSPER is especially important. The tool's ability to identify edges present in the RFC text, that were not present in the diagrams, is particularly strong.
    *   **User-Friendly Interface:** The interactive interface allows for easy exploration, annotation, and verification of extracted information. The provenance tracking (linking diagram elements to RFC text) is a standout feature.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The tool's performance is dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLM (GPT-4.1 in this case). The choice of the LLM could introduce biases and limitations. There's also an implicit assumption of the LLM's reliability. The work also does not measure the latency of the tool.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Scope:** While the case studies are useful, a more systematic evaluation (e.g., measuring the improvement in comprehension or reduction in implementation errors using RFSeek) would strengthen the paper. Quantifying the benefits is a clear area for future work.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Complexity:** The prompt engineering involved to get the LLM to extract the information accurately is likely to be non-trivial, though the paper attempts to describe their reasoning, prompting engineering could be considered a weakness.

*   **Potential Influence:** RFSeek could be used by protocol developers, security researchers, and network engineers to gain a deeper understanding of internet protocols, identify potential vulnerabilities, and ensure correct implementation. The approach could also influence the way that future RFCs are written, encouraging clearer and more comprehensive specifications. The identification of inconsistencies and ambiguities in existing RFCs could lead to revisions and improvements to these standards.

**Justification of Score:**

Despite the reliance on LLMs, RFSeek provides a novel and significant tool for protocol analysis. The approach is well-defined, the evaluation is convincing, and the user interface is thoughtfully designed. While a more comprehensive evaluation would strengthen the paper, the existing results demonstrate the tool's potential to improve protocol understanding and reduce implementation errors. The clear emphasis on the provenance of the extracted elements is a significant factor. Given these factors, I believe a score of 8 is justified.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.NI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Mask Consistency Regularization in Object Removal
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10259v1)
- **Authors**: Hua Yuan, Jin Yuan, Yicheng Jiang, Yao Zhang, Xin Geng, Yong Rui
- **Abstract**: Object removal, a challenging task within image inpainting, involves seamlessly filling the removed region with content that matches the surrounding context. Despite advancements in diffusion models, current methods still face two critical challenges. The first is mask hallucination, where the model generates irrelevant or spurious content inside the masked region, and the second is mask-shape bias, where the model fills the masked area with an object that mimics the mask's shape rather than surrounding content. To address these issues, we propose Mask Consistency Regularization (MCR), a novel training strategy designed specifically for object removal tasks. During training, our approach introduces two mask perturbations: dilation and reshape, enforcing consistency between the outputs of these perturbed branches and the original mask. The dilated masks help align the model's output with the surrounding content, while reshaped masks encourage the model to break the mask-shape bias. This combination of strategies enables MCR to produce more robust and contextually coherent inpainting results. Our experiments demonstrate that MCR significantly reduces hallucinations and mask-shape bias, leading to improved performance in object removal.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the information provided.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of object removal via image inpainting using diffusion models. It identifies two key challenges in this context: mask hallucination (generating irrelevant content within the masked region) and mask-shape bias (the model filling the area based on the mask's shape rather than the surrounding context). The authors propose a novel training strategy called Mask Consistency Regularization (MCR). MCR introduces mask perturbations (dilation and reshaping) during training and enforces consistency between the outputs generated from the original and perturbed masks. This encourages the model to be less reliant on the mask's geometry and more aligned with the surrounding context, leading to reduced hallucinations and improved object removal performance. The method builds on Stable Diffusion XL (SDXL) with a ControlNet module and achieves improvements over existing baselines on benchmark datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the problems of mask hallucination and mask-shape bias, which are crucial issues in mask-based image inpainting. This demonstrates a strong understanding of the existing limitations.
    *   **Novelty of Approach:** The proposed Mask Consistency Regularization (MCR) is a reasonably novel training strategy. The idea of using mask perturbations to enforce consistency and reduce reliance on mask geometry is interesting and addresses the identified challenges directly. The combination of dilation and reshaping provides complementary benefits.
    *   **Solid Experimental Results:** The experimental results show that MCR outperforms several baselines, including LaMa and SDXL-I, on both BrushBench and a custom 300-sample removal test dataset. The consistent improvement across different metrics (FID, PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS, CMMD) lends credibility to the claims. The ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of each perturbation strategy (dilation and reshaping).
    *   **Well-Written and Structured:** The paper is well-written, with a clear structure and logical flow. The method is described in sufficient detail, and the experiments are presented in a clear and understandable manner.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the method shows improvements, it's built upon existing frameworks (SDXL, ControlNet). The core idea, while novel in this *specific* context, draws inspiration from consistency regularization used in semi-supervised learning. The overall improvement may be seen as incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   **Limited Scope of Perturbations:** While dilation and reshaping are reasonable choices for mask perturbations, the paper doesn't explore a wide range of perturbation strategies. There might be other, more effective ways to perturb the masks.
    *   **Custom Dataset:** Using a self-constructed dataset for evaluation, while justified due to the lack of existing datasets with ground truth, can also introduce bias. It would be stronger if evaluations were also carried out on a more established image manipulation dataset that included similar object removal-related tasks.
    *   **No Theoretical Analysis:** There is no formal theoretical justification for why MCR works. While the intuition is sound, a mathematical explanation could strengthen the paper.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses an important problem in image inpainting.
    *   The proposed MCR method offers a practical solution to mitigate the limitations of existing diffusion-based inpainting techniques.
    *   The method can potentially be used as a training technique to enhance object removal performance.
    *   The paper introduces a custom removal dataset, but it may not be sufficient to be considered a strong contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonable and well-executed solution to a relevant problem in object removal. The approach is somewhat novel, but it builds upon existing techniques. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method, but the degree of improvement is not groundbreaking. The significance of the work lies in its practical utility and the contribution of a practical technique that performs better than existing methods. The lack of more extensive ablation studies or theoretical analysis somewhat limits the overall impact.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

A score of 6.5 reflects the paper's positive contributions (novelty of MCR, solid results, clear presentation) while acknowledging its limitations (incremental improvement, limited scope of perturbations, custom dataset). It is above average, indicating a worthwhile contribution to the field, but it does not reach the level of an exceptional or transformative work that would merit a higher score. The novelty is moderate, the improvements are noticeable but not transformative, and the custom dataset while justified and used properly, could be a limited validation. The lack of more rigorous testing of a variety of loss functions or mask perturbation methods, or theoretical justification, also limits the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### MagicMirror: A Large-Scale Dataset and Benchmark for Fine-Grained Artifacts Assessment in Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10260v1)
- **Authors**: Jia Wang, Jie Hu, Xiaoqi Ma, Hanghang Ma, Yanbing Zeng, Xiaoming Wei
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image (T2I) generation has achieved remarkable progress in instruction following and aesthetics. However, a persistent challenge is the prevalence of physical artifacts, such as anatomical and structural flaws, which severely degrade perceptual quality and limit application. Given the diversity and complexity of these artifacts, a systematic and fine-grained evaluation framework is required, which is lacking in current benchmarks. To fill this gap, we introduce MagicMirror, a comprehensive framework for artifacts assessment. We first establish a detailed taxonomy of generated image artifacts. Guided by this taxonomy, we manually annotate MagicData340K, the first human-annotated large-scale dataset of 340K generated images with fine-grained artifact labels. Building on this dataset, we train MagicAssessor, a Vision-Language Model (VLM) that provides detailed assessments and corresponding labels. To overcome challenges like class imbalance and reward hacking, we design a novel data sampling strategy and a multi-level reward system for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Finally, we leverage MagicAssessor to construct MagicBench, an automated benchmark for evaluating the image artifacts of current T2I models. Our evaluation with MagicBench reveals that despite their widespread adoption, even top-tier models like GPT-image-1 are consistently plagued by significant artifacts, highlighting artifact reduction as a critical frontier for future T2I development. Project page: https://wj-inf.github.io/MagicMirror-page/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MagicMirror, a framework for assessing artifacts in text-to-image (T2I) generation.  It addresses the lack of fine-grained evaluation in existing benchmarks by presenting a detailed taxonomy of image artifacts categorized into object anatomy, attributes, and interactions. The core components of MagicMirror are:

1.  **MagicData340K:** A large-scale, human-annotated dataset of 340K generated images with fine-grained artifact labels based on the proposed taxonomy.
2.  **MagicAssessor:** A Vision-Language Model (VLM) trained to assess images and provide detailed assessments using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a modified version for the reward system and sampling strategy.
3.  **MagicBench:** An automated benchmark leveraging MagicAssessor to evaluate the image artifacts produced by current T2I models.

The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that even state-of-the-art models like GPT-image-1 still exhibit significant artifacts.  The authors emphasize artifact reduction as a critical area for future T2I development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several novel aspects:
    *   The detailed taxonomy of image artifacts is a valuable contribution, providing a structured way to analyze and categorize defects.
    *   MagicData340K represents a substantial, human-annotated dataset specifically designed for artifact evaluation. The scale and fine-grained labels address a significant gap in resources for this task.
    *   The adaptation of GRPO with custom data sampling and reward system to overcome the challenges of data imbalance and reward hacking is an interesting methodological contribution.
    *  MagicBench provides a standardized and automated way to benchmark and compare the artifacts generated by different T2I models.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a crucial, yet often overlooked, problem in T2I generation – the presence of physical artifacts.  By providing a comprehensive framework for assessment, it contributes to:
    *   **Improved Evaluation:** MagicMirror provides a more nuanced and informative evaluation of T2I models than existing benchmarks, focusing on a previously under-evaluated aspect.
    *   **Targeted Development:**  By identifying specific types of artifacts, the framework enables researchers to target their efforts on reducing specific defects in T2I models.
    *   **Practical Applications:** Reducing artifacts is crucial for improving the reliability and usability of T2I models in real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly presents the problem, methodology, and results.
    *   The experiments are thorough, evaluating a range of T2I models and providing detailed performance analysis.
    *   The analysis of ablation studies provides valuable insights into the effectiveness of different components of MagicAssessor.
    *  The work introduces a new and needed benchmark in an area that is often side-lined.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the taxonomy is detailed, it relies on human annotation, which can be subjective. It is not clear if the taxonomy and corresponding annotation could be generalized to other types of artefacts, beyond the scope of the current study.
    *   The reliance on a single VLM (MagicAssessor) for automated evaluation may introduce biases or limitations in the types of artifacts that are detected.  The paper could benefit from exploring ways to incorporate ensemble methods or other techniques to reduce the dependence on a single evaluator.
    *   The paper could explore potential methods to reduce the artefacts. Although it highlights the need for artefact reduction, it does not offer guidance or suggestions for future development.

*   **Potential Influence:**  MagicMirror has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for artifact evaluation in T2I generation. The dataset and taxonomy could serve as valuable resources for future research in this area. By focusing on artifact reduction, the paper may inspire new approaches to T2I model training and architecture.  The framework helps to accelerate and refine approaches to address a long-standing and critical challenge in T2I.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing an important gap in the T2I field. It provides a concrete framework, a large dataset, and a benchmark that allows for a targeted and fine-grained evaluation of artifact generation. The paper's thoroughness, clarity, and potential influence warrant a good score. While the dependence on human annotation and a single VLM evaluator limit the scope, the overall impact of this work is significant.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### SignClip: Leveraging Mouthing Cues for Sign Language Translation by Multimodal Contrastive Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10266v1)
- **Authors**: Wenfang Wu, Tingting Yuan, Yupeng Li, Daling Wang, Xiaoming Fu
- **Abstract**: Sign language translation (SLT) aims to translate natural language from sign language videos, serving as a vital bridge for inclusive communication. While recent advances leverage powerful visual backbones and large language models, most approaches mainly focus on manual signals (hand gestures) and tend to overlook non-manual cues like mouthing. In fact, mouthing conveys essential linguistic information in sign languages and plays a crucial role in disambiguating visually similar signs. In this paper, we propose SignClip, a novel framework to improve the accuracy of sign language translation. It fuses manual and non-manual cues, specifically spatial gesture and lip movement features. Besides, SignClip introduces a hierarchical contrastive learning framework with multi-level alignment objectives, ensuring semantic consistency across sign-lip and visual-text modalities. Extensive experiments on two benchmark datasets, PHOENIX14T and How2Sign, demonstrate the superiority of our approach. For example, on PHOENIX14T, in the Gloss-free setting, SignClip surpasses the previous state-of-the-art model SpaMo, improving BLEU-4 from 24.32 to 24.71, and ROUGE from 46.57 to 48.38.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SignClip, a novel framework for Sign Language Translation (SLT) that emphasizes the integration of mouthing cues (non-manual signals) along with hand gestures (manual signals). SignClip uses a dual-stream architecture to independently encode visual information from the full frame (for gesture) and the localized mouth region. It uses a gated fusion module to combine these modalities. The model is trained with a hierarchical contrastive learning framework, aligning spatial (gesture) and mouthing features, as well as aligning visual features with textual semantics.  Experiments on PHOENIX14T and How2Sign datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of SignClip, showing improvements over existing state-of-the-art gloss-free SLT methods, especially in scenarios where mouthing is crucial for disambiguation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the explicit and structured incorporation of mouthing cues in a gloss-free SLT system. While existing SLT systems acknowledge the importance of non-manual features, SignClip provides a dedicated architecture and training regime (dual-stream encoder, gated fusion, contrastive learning) to leverage these cues.  The contrastive learning framework, specifically the spatial-mouthing contrastive loss, is also a contribution. However, the use of visual backbones (CLIP ViT, Av-HuBERT) and language models (Flan-T5) themselves isn't novel; the integration strategy is what distinguishes the work. The scaling on scales feature enrichment is borrowed from SpaMo, and LoRA is a known technique for fine-tuning.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a known limitation of existing SLT systems: their over-reliance on hand gestures and neglect of facial expressions, particularly mouthing, which are essential for accurate translation, especially in disambiguating similar signs. By demonstrating consistent improvements on standard benchmark datasets, particularly in a gloss-free setting, the paper provides evidence that incorporating mouthing cues is a worthwhile direction for future research. The improvements, while not drastically large in some cases, are consistent and statistically significant, suggesting a real benefit. The ablation studies further reinforce the value of the individual components. The qualitative analysis provides concrete examples of how SignClip improves the translation.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and motivation.
    * Well-defined architecture with a focus on incorporating mouthing cues.
    * Effective use of contrastive learning to align different modalities.
    * Consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines on two benchmark datasets.
    * Thorough ablation studies to evaluate the contribution of individual components.
    * Qualitative analysis provides insights into the model's behavior.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The reliance on pre-trained models (CLIP, Av-HuBERT, Flan-T5) limits the potential for further improvement through end-to-end training of the entire system.  Although LoRA helps, a fully end-to-end approach *might* yield even better results.  It would be good to have a discussion on that.
    * The improvements, while consistent, could be more significant. While the relative improvements compared to SpaMo are noteworthy, in absolute terms they are somewhat modest. This raises questions about the generalizability of the approach to other datasets or sign languages.
    * While the paper focuses on mouthing, there are other important non-manual features (eye gaze, head nods, etc.) that are not addressed. The paper would benefit from a discussion of how SignClip could be extended to incorporate these other cues. The limitations section could have been more thorough.
    * The computational cost and resource requirements of the model should be discussed more explicitly, especially considering the use of multiple pre-trained models.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in SLT by highlighting the importance of mouthing cues and providing a practical framework for incorporating them.  It could also inspire further exploration of contrastive learning techniques for multimodal alignment in SLT. The dual-stream architecture with gated fusion could serve as a template for future models.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:** SignClip makes a valuable contribution to the field of SLT by effectively incorporating mouthing cues, an often-overlooked aspect of sign language. The dual-stream architecture, gated fusion, and contrastive learning objectives are well-designed and lead to consistent improvements on benchmark datasets.  While the absolute improvements aren't huge and limitations exist regarding the breadth of non-manual signals considered and the end-to-end trainability, the work is novel and significant enough to warrant a score of 7.  The study has the potential to influence future work by emphasizing the importance of mouthing and providing a solid foundation for future research on multimodal SLT. It's not breaking new ground on the *component* techniques utilized but shows a good engineering effort to assemble them into a novel approach with demonstrated benefits.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### URL2Graph++: Unified Semantic-Structural-Character Learning for Malicious URL Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10287v1)
- **Authors**: Ye Tian, Yifan Jia, Yanbin Wang, Jianguo Sun, Zhiquan Liu, Xiaowen Ling
- **Abstract**: Malicious URL detection remains a major challenge in cybersecurity, primarily due to two factors: (1) the exponential growth of the Internet has led to an immense diversity of URLs, making generalized detection increasingly difficult; and (2) attackers are increasingly employing sophisticated obfuscation techniques to evade detection. We advocate that addressing these challenges fundamentally requires: (1) obtaining semantic understanding to improve generalization across vast and diverse URL sets, and (2) accurately modeling contextual relationships within the structural composition of URLs. In this paper, we propose a novel malicious URL detection method combining multi-granularity graph learning with semantic embedding to jointly capture semantic, character-level, and structural features for robust URL analysis. To model internal dependencies within URLs, we first construct dual-granularity URL graphs at both subword and character levels, where nodes represent URL tokens/characters and edges encode co-occurrence relationships. To obtain fine-grained embeddings, we initialize node representations using a character-level convolutional network. The two graphs are then processed through jointly trained Graph Convolutional Networks to learn consistent graph-level representations, enabling the model to capture complementary structural features that reflect co-occurrence patterns and character-level dependencies. Furthermore, we employ BERT to derive semantic representations of URLs for semantically aware understanding. Finally, we introduce a gated dynamic fusion network to combine the semantically enriched BERT representations with the jointly optimized graph vectors, further enhancing detection performance. We extensively evaluate our method across multiple challenging dimensions. Results show our method exceeds SOTA performance, including against large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "URL2Graph++: Unified Semantic-Structural-Character Learning for Malicious URL Detection":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces URL2Graph++, a novel framework for malicious URL detection.  It addresses limitations in existing methods by combining multi-granularity graph learning with semantic embeddings.  The method constructs dual-granularity URL graphs (subword and character level) to model internal URL dependencies and leverages a character-level CNN to initialize node representations. Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) are trained on these graphs to learn structural features, while BERT is used to derive semantic representations. A gated dynamic fusion network combines these representations, enhancing detection performance. The approach is evaluated on real-world datasets, demonstrating improved generalization, robustness to character confusion, and performance on short URLs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel combination of techniques, specifically the dual-granularity graph approach within the URL analysis domain. Existing graph-based URL detection mainly focused on inter-domain or host-level relationships, not internal URL structure. Character-level CNN use exists, but the connection to graph-based representation learning is a contribution. The gated fusion is a standard approach, but its application in this context is practical and enhances the overall system. However, the individual components (BERT, GCNs, CNNs) are not novel in and of themselves. The key novelty is in their integration and application to the specific problem of malicious URL detection.

*   **Significance:** The malicious URL detection problem is a crucial cybersecurity challenge. The paper demonstrably improves performance over existing state-of-the-art methods, including large language models, in several critical aspects, such as short URL detection and robustness to adversarial attacks. The extensive experimental evaluation across diverse datasets (with varying class imbalances and distributions) strengthens the significance of the results. The real-world focus and consideration of issues like distribution shift are particularly valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides a thorough experimental analysis, including in-distribution performance, cross-dataset generalization, ablation studies, adversarial robustness testing, and short URL analysis. This comprehensive approach strengthens the validity of the claims.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Motivation:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing methods and motivates the need for a combined semantic-structural-character-level approach.
    *   **Performance Gains:** The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements over established baselines, particularly in challenging scenarios.
    *   **Code Availability:** The public availability of the code enhances reproducibility and allows for further research and adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Complexity:** While the paper addresses scalability using mini-batching, it doesn't fully explore the computational cost of the proposed approach, especially regarding the dual-graph construction and BERT integration. This is a critical factor for real-world deployment.
    *   **Limited Adversarial Scenarios:** While adversarial robustness is tested, the attack surface could be explored further. Focusing on more sophisticated adversarial techniques (e.g., gradient-based attacks, semantic-preserving attacks) would provide a more rigorous assessment.
    *   **BERT Fine-tuning Details**: The description of BERT's integration could be more detailed. The paper mentions optional fine-tuning, but the impact and methodology for fine-tuning are not deeply explored.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by highlighting the benefits of combined semantic and structural learning in malicious URL detection. The dual-granularity graph approach could inspire new methods for modeling other types of network security data.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 7 is justified. While the individual components are not groundbreaking, the innovative integration of dual-granularity graph learning with semantic embeddings, the thorough evaluation, and the demonstrated performance gains contribute significantly to the field. The weaknesses related to computational complexity and limited exploration of sophisticated adversarial attacks slightly detract from the overall impact, preventing a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Morality of Probability: How Implicit Moral Biases in LLMs May Shape the Future of Human-AI Symbiosis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10297v1)
- **Authors**: Eoin O'Doherty, Nicole Weinrauch, Andrew Talone, Uri Klempner, Xiaoyuan Yi, Xing Xie, Yi Zeng
- **Abstract**: Artificial intelligence (AI) is advancing at a pace that raises urgent questions about how to align machine decision-making with human moral values. This working paper investigates how leading AI systems prioritize moral outcomes and what this reveals about the prospects for human-AI symbiosis. We address two central questions: (1) What moral values do state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) implicitly favour when confronted with dilemmas? (2) How do differences in model architecture, cultural origin, and explainability affect these moral preferences? To explore these questions, we conduct a quantitative experiment with six LLMs, ranking and scoring outcomes across 18 dilemmas representing five moral frameworks. Our findings uncover strikingly consistent value biases. Across all models, Care and Virtue values outcomes were rated most moral, while libertarian choices were consistently penalized. Reasoning-enabled models exhibited greater sensitivity to context and provided richer explanations, whereas non-reasoning models produced more uniform but opaque judgments. This research makes three contributions: (i) Empirically, it delivers a large-scale comparison of moral reasoning across culturally distinct LLMs; (ii) Theoretically, it links probabilistic model behaviour with underlying value encodings; (iii) Practically, it highlights the need for explainability and cultural awareness as critical design principles to guide AI toward a transparent, aligned, and symbiotic future.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper "The Morality of Probability: How Implicit Moral Biases in LLMs May Shape the Future of Human-AI Symbiosis." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the implicit moral biases present in large language models (LLMs) and their potential impact on human-AI symbiosis. The authors conduct a quantitative experiment with six LLMs (US and Chinese models) using 18 dilemma scenarios representing five moral frameworks (Utilitarian, Deontological, Virtue, Care, and Libertarian).  They analyze the models' rankings and scores of outcomes across these dilemmas, investigating how model architecture, cultural origin, and explainability affect moral preferences. The study finds consistent value biases, with "Care" and "Virtue" values consistently rated higher than "Libertarian" values.  Reasoning-enabled models show more context sensitivity and richer explanations, while non-reasoning models produce more uniform judgments. The paper highlights the importance of explainability and cultural awareness in AI design to foster transparent, aligned, and symbiotic human-AI interaction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Empirical Contribution:** The paper presents a well-designed quantitative experiment comparing moral reasoning across a range of culturally distinct LLMs. This provides a valuable empirical basis for discussing and theorizing about AI value alignment. The dataset creation involving Claude is an interesting approach.
*   **Clear Research Questions:** The paper focuses on specific, well-defined research questions, making the investigation targeted and the findings more impactful.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The authors conduct a thorough analysis, including descriptive statistics, statistical tests (ANOVA, Kruskal-Wallis), and interpretability techniques (SHAP, MDI) to explore the data from multiple angles.
*   **Discussion of Limitations:** The authors explicitly acknowledge the limitations of their methodology, including the simplified nature of the dilemmas, potential biases in training data, and constraints on the types of model tested, which enhances the transparency of the research. The discussion of "in-context scheming" and its implications for trust is well-integrated.
*   **Links Theory and Practice:** The paper connects the empirical findings with theoretical discussions on AI alignment, explainability, and human-AI symbiosis, which bridges technical AI research with broader ethical and societal implications.
*   **Focus on a pressing issue:** The paper studies a significant problem, namely the problem of value alignment and the difficulty of the task in the wake of rapidly improving and deploying AI systems.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplistic Dilemmas:** The dilemmas, although covering various themes, are necessarily simplified representations of real-world moral complexities. This might limit the generalizability of the findings. Although acknowledged, this constraint significantly impact the findings.
*   **Limited Model Diversity:** While the paper includes models from US and Chinese developers, the overall number of models (six) is relatively small, and there could be other architectural and cultural factors not captured in this selection. While this is stated to be a limitation, a more comprehensive investigation of models could impact the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Potential for Prompt Engineering Bias:** While the paper attempts to minimize prompt bias, there is always the potential that the specific phrasing of the prompts could influence the models' responses in subtle ways.
*   **Overclaim of Cultural Difference:** While the paper shows interesting differences between US and Chinese models, further analysis is required to determine the validity of cultural influences, such as analysing model architectures and training data.
*   **Lack of Causation:** The analysis is largely correlational, and the authors do not establish causal relationships between model architecture, cultural origin, and moral preferences, limiting the strength of the conclusions.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper offers a useful empirical contribution to the field by quantitatively assessing moral biases in LLMs. The comparative analysis of US and Chinese models is particularly valuable, as it provides insights into the potential cultural influences on AI development. Furthermore, the paper's discussion of explainability and the problem of "scheming" highlights critical challenges for building trustworthy AI systems. This work furthers our understanding of the biases that underpin LLMs and how they can impact the future of society. It is a useful, but limited, paper that should be a stepping stone for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6.5/10**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution with its empirical assessment of moral biases in LLMs and the comparisons among cultural models. Its comprehensive analysis, limitations, and connections to theory all add value. However, the limited complexity of the scenarios, size of the model datasets and inability to confirm cultural impacts are all limitations. A higher score would require a more robust study. It's a solid piece of work, but not groundbreaking. It would be useful to expand on these findings by diving further into the training data and cultural dimensions.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Adversarial robustness through Lipschitz-Guided Stochastic Depth in Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10298v1)
- **Authors**: Laith Nayal, Mahmoud Mousatat, Bader Rasheed
- **Abstract**: Deep neural networks and Vision Transformers achieve state-of-the-art performance in computer vision but are highly vulnerable to adversarial perturbations. Standard defenses often incur high computational cost or lack formal guarantees. We propose a Lipschitz-guided stochastic depth (DropPath) method, where drop probabilities increase with depth to control the effective Lipschitz constant of the network. This approach regularizes deeper layers, improving robustness while preserving clean accuracy and reducing computation. Experiments on CIFAR-10 with ViT-Tiny show that our custom depth-dependent schedule maintains near-baseline clean accuracy, enhances robustness under FGSM, PGD-20, and AutoAttack, and significantly reduces FLOPs compared to baseline and linear DropPath schedules.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel approach to improve the adversarial robustness of deep neural networks, specifically Vision Transformers (ViTs), using a Lipschitz-guided stochastic depth (DropPath) method.  The key idea is to vary the dropout probabilities across layers based on the network's estimated Lipschitz constant, thereby regularizing deeper layers, which are assumed to contribute more to vulnerability.  The method aims to enhance robustness against adversarial attacks while preserving clean accuracy and reducing computational cost (FLOPs). Experiments on CIFAR-10 with ViT-Tiny demonstrate that their custom depth schedule maintains clean accuracy, improves robustness against FGSM, PGD-20, and AutoAttack, and significantly reduces FLOPs compared to baseline and linear DropPath schedules. The paper also includes an empirical evaluation of local Lipschitz constants to show that the proposed method effectively targets deeper layers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper presents a reasonable combination of existing techniques (stochastic depth/DropPath and Lipschitz constant control), but the core novelty lies in the specific application of Lipschitz-guided schedules for *controlling the dropout probabilities in a deep neural network, in particular ViTs*.  While the general idea of regularizing deeper layers to improve robustness is not entirely new, the use of a Lipschitz estimate for guiding *stochastic depth* is a specific contribution. The schedule used for the drop probabilities is also relatively simple. It's an incremental improvement rather than a paradigm shift.

* **Significance:**  The significance stems from the potential for a more efficient trade-off between robustness, accuracy, and computational cost. The experimental results show promising improvements in robustness compared to a standard linear drop path schedule *without* sacrificing clean accuracy and with significantly reduced FLOPs. This is particularly important for deploying robust models in resource-constrained environments.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Empirical results:** The experiments on CIFAR-10 are compelling and clearly demonstrate the benefits of the proposed approach in terms of robustness and computational efficiency. The comparisons with a baseline and linear drop schedule provide good context.
    * **Clear problem formulation:** The paper clearly states the problem, the proposed solution, and the evaluation metrics.
    * **Lipschitz interpretation:** The idea of using Lipschitz constants to guide regularization is theoretically sound and helps explain why the proposed method works. The empirical estimation of local Lipschitz constants provides further support.
    * **Reduced FLOPs:** The significant reduction in FLOPs is a major advantage, suggesting that this method could be useful in practice.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Evaluation:** The experiments are limited to CIFAR-10 and ViT-Tiny.  It's unclear how well the method would generalize to larger datasets (e.g., ImageNet) or different architectures (e.g., CNNs). This is a significant limitation.
    * **Simplicity of Lipschitz guiding:** The paper relies on a rather simplistic depth-dependent schedule guided by a rough interpretation of a Lipschitz constant. A more sophisticated approach to estimating and controlling the Lipschitz constant could potentially yield even better results.
    * **Global Lipschitz guarantee is lacking:**  The paper acknowledges the lack of a formal guarantee on the global Lipschitz constant. This is a theoretical weakness. The local Lipschitz estimates are helpful, but not conclusive. The statement in the limitations section that deeper layers are "effectively regularized" is based on the empirical local Lipschitz estimates which are actually *higher* on average. The argument rests more on performance measures than a concrete Lipschitz constant bound.
    * **Incremental nature:** The overall approach feels somewhat incremental, combining well-known techniques in a specific way. The novelty is not groundbreaking.

* **Potential Influence:** If the method generalizes well to other datasets and architectures, it could become a standard technique for improving the efficiency of adversarially robust models. The focus on balancing robustness, accuracy, and computational cost is highly relevant to practical applications.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and the incremental nature of the novelty, a score of 6 seems appropriate. While the paper makes a practically significant contribution by improving the trade-off between robustness, accuracy, and FLOPs, the limited evaluation and lack of a formal global Lipschitz guarantee prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The CIFAR-10 results are promising, but significantly more extensive experiments are needed to demonstrate its broader applicability.
